          - region "Article content" [ref=e96]:
            - generic [ref=e97]:
              - generic [ref=e98]:
                - heading "Abstract" [level=2] [ref=e99]: Abstract
                - paragraph [ref=e100]: Oxidation–reduction (redox) reactions are central to the existence of life. Reactive species of oxygen, nitrogen and sulfur mediate redox control of a wide range of essential cellular processes. Yet, excessive levels of oxidants are associated with ageing and many diseases, including cardiological and neurodegenerative diseases, and cancer. Hence, maintaining the fine-tuned steady-state balance of reactive species production and removal is essential. Here, we discuss new insights into the dynamic maintenance of redox homeostasis (that is, redox homeodynamics) and the principles underlying biological redox organization, termed the ‘redox code’. We survey how redox changes result in stress responses by hormesis mechanisms, and how the lifelong cumulative exposure to environmental agents, termed the ‘exposome’, is communicated to cells through redox signals. Better understanding of the molecular and cellular basis of redox biology will guide novel redox medicine approaches aimed at preventing and treating diseases associated with disturbed redox regulation.
              - generic [ref=e101]:
                - heading "Introduction" [level=2] [ref=e102]: Introduction
                - paragraph [ref=e103]:
                  - text: Oxidation–reduction (redox) reactions denote electron transfer reactions between chemical species. They serve as a basis of life processes, from the capture of solar energy in oxygen-generating photosynthesis to driving cell metabolism
                  - superscript [ref=e104]:
                    - link "1" [ref=e105] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R1"
                  - text: . Various redox reactions steer and control biological processes in what is known as redox regulation. A central role in redox regulation is performed by sulfur-containing and selenium-containing amino acid residues in proteins
                  - superscript [ref=e106]:
                    - link "2" [ref=e107] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R2"
                  - text: ", whose reversible oxidation by physiological oxidants, such as hydrogen peroxide (H"
                  - subscript [ref=e108]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e109]: "2"
                  - text: ), serves as a switch to control and modulate enzyme activity and protein function. These redox switches allow cells and organisms to respond to various different stimuli, for example, growth factors, toxins and changes in nutrient availability
                  - superscript [ref=e110]:
                    - link "3" [ref=e111] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R3"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "4" [ref=e112] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R4"
                  - text: . On the basis of pioneering early work
                  - superscript [ref=e113]:
                    - link "5" [ref=e114] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R5"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "6" [ref=e115] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R6"
                  - text: ", progress in molecular cell biology techniques has provided astounding advances in delineating the different functions of reactive species as mediators of redox-regulated processes"
                  - superscript [ref=e116]:
                    - link "7" [ref=e117] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R7"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "8" [ref=e118] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R8"
                  - text: . The focus of this Review is primarily on reactive oxygen species (ROS)
                  - superscript [ref=e119]:
                    - link "3" [ref=e120] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R3"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "9" [ref=e121] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R9"
                  - text: ", which encompass radical and non-radical derivatives, including superoxide (O"
                  - subscript [ref=e122]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e123]: •−
                  - text: ) and H
                  - subscript [ref=e124]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e125]: "2"
                  - text: . We will discuss how H
                  - subscript [ref=e126]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e127]: "2"
                  - text: ", generated by redox pathways in mitochondria, the endoplasmic reticulum, peroxisomes, the nucleus, the cytosol or at the plasma membrane, serves as a second messenger that triggers cell signals through reversible oxidation of protein cysteine residues"
                  - superscript [ref=e128]:
                    - link "3" [ref=e129] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R3"
                  - text: . At the organismal level, H
                  - subscript [ref=e130]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e131]: "2"
                  - text: signals are required for embryogenesis and tissue development, cell migration, neural activity, wound healing and many more processes. Notably, reactive species of nitrogen (RNS, for example, nitric oxide (•NO))
                  - superscript [ref=e132]:
                    - link "10" [ref=e133] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R10"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "11" [ref=e134] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R11"
                  - text: ", sulfur (RSS; for example, hydrogen sulfide (H"
                  - subscript [ref=e135]: "2"
                  - text: S))
                  - superscript [ref=e136]:
                    - link "12" [ref=e137] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R12"
                  - text: ", electrophiles (RES; for example, 4-hydroxy-2-nonenal)"
                  - superscript [ref=e138]:
                    - link "13" [ref=e139] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R13"
                  - text: or carbonyls
                  - superscript [ref=e140]:
                    - link "14" [ref=e141] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R14"
                  - text: ", each of which describe a wide range of chemical species with different properties and cell interactions"
                  - superscript [ref=e142]:
                    - link "8" [ref=e143] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R8"
                  - text: ", also fulfil signalling roles but will not be discussed in detail."
                - paragraph [ref=e144]:
                  - text: The scope of this Review is confined to mammalian cell metabolism without detailed inclusion of other large fields of biological redox science such as in plants
                  - superscript [ref=e145]:
                    - link "15" [ref=e146] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R15"
                    - text: –
                    - link "17" [ref=e147] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R17"
                  - text: . We discuss novel developments in the field of redox biology by presenting an integrated perspective on the interorganelle and intercellular signalling properties of oxidants, foremost H
                  - subscript [ref=e148]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e149]: "2"
                  - text: ", and their importance in the control of cellular functions"
                  - superscript [ref=e150]:
                    - link "18" [ref=e151] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R18"
                  - text: . We address recent concepts in redox homeodynamics, which are characterized by continuous redox sensing, activation of redox signal transduction and subsequent signal translation into activation of multiple types of cellular stress responses. These responses involve changes in the activity of redox-regulated proteins (that is, the redox proteome) and culminate in changes in the epigenetic landscape and gene expression. We will discuss hormesis (from the Greek
                  - emphasis [ref=e152]: hormaein
                  - text: ": ‘to set in motion’), which describes the overall biological adaptive response after exposure to low doses of stressors (for example, oxidants), and explore the newly recognized roles of non-coding RNAs (ncRNAs) in redox regulation. We will present emerging knowledge on the multifaceted interaction with the external environment, collectively termed the exposome"
                  - superscript [ref=e153]:
                    - link "19" [ref=e154] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R19"
                  - text: ", and conclude by considering the evolving field of redox medicine and give perspectives on existing knowledge gaps."
              - generic [ref=e155]:
                - heading "Dynamics in redox homeostasis and the redox code" [level=2] [ref=e156]: Dynamics in redox homeostasis and the redox code
                - paragraph [ref=e157]:
                  - text: Redox homeostasis denotes the maintenance of the balance between oxidants and antioxidants. Although excessive oxidant production causes oxidative damage to cellular biomolecules and results in oxidative distress, lack of adequate oxidant levels impairs crucial signalling processes and results in reductive distress
                  - superscript [ref=e158]:
                    - link "20" [ref=e159] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R20"
                  - text: . The balance between oxidants and antioxidants is obtained by redox regulation, which encompasses redox sensing, redox signalling, redox responses and feedback control pathways (
                  - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e160] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#F1"
                  - text: ). Reduction of oxidative modification is driven by the reductive power of NADPH and NADH, which serve as ‘ideal packets of diffusible two-electron transfer currency’
                  - superscript [ref=e161]:
                    - link "1" [ref=e162] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R1"
                  - text: to maintain the redox state of hundreds of different proteins and small molecules. The link between NADPH and cellular protein thiols, the primary targets of cellular redox modifications, is mediated by the thioredoxin and glutathione (GSH) systems, catalysed by thioredoxin reductases and GSH disulfide reductase, respectively
                  - superscript [ref=e163]:
                    - link "21" [ref=e164] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R21"
                  - text: .
                - figure [ref=e165]:
                  - heading "Fig. 1 |. Principles of redox regulation." [level=3] [ref=e166]
                  - paragraph [ref=e167]:
                    - img "Fig. 1 |" [ref=e168]
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e170] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: figure/F1/
                  - paragraph [ref=e172]:
                    - text: The process of redox regulation begins with initial causal input cues (for example, endogenous cues such as hormonal stimuli or exogenous input from nutrients). Endogenous and exogenous redox signal generation causes the production of reactive species of oxygen (ROS), nitrogen (RNS) and sulfur (RSS). These redox signals can be propagated by superoxide (O
                    - subscript [ref=e173]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e174]: •−
                    - text: ), hydrogen peroxide (H
                    - subscript [ref=e175]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e176]: "2"
                    - text: ), nitric oxide (•NO) and hydrogen sulfide (H
                    - subscript [ref=e177]: "2"
                    - text: S), which are produced by NADPH oxidase (NOX), the electron transport chain (ETC) and other FAD-dependent mitochondrial dehydrogenases, nitric oxide synthase (NOS) and cystathionine γ-lyase (CSE), respectively. Redox signals can also be mediated through electrophiles, lipid hydroperoxides and nitroalkenes. Transduction of the redox signal leads to the modulation of the redox proteome and metabolome. The redox signal is propagated through the site-specific oxidation and reduction of cysteine switches, also called redox switches. As discussed in detail in
                    - link "Box 2" [ref=e178] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#BX2"
                    - text: ", the redox switches are subject to reversible oxidation and reduction, either through S-sulfenylation, S-glutathionylation, S-nitrosylation or other redox-sensitive modifications, which transmit the redox signal. Redox signal propagation is also mediated through the redoxin family of enzymes (thioredoxins, glutaredoxins and peroxiredoxins), which are activated and/or deactivated by oxidation and reduction of catalytic thiols. The stress-sensing transcription factors such as nuclear factor erythroid 2-related factor (NRF2), nuclear factor-κB (NF-κB), hypoxia-inducible factor 1 (HIF-1) or heat shock factor 1 (HSF-1) are also redox sensors and are activated or deactivated by cysteine thiol oxidation and reduction, which affect their ability to activate the expression of genes required to invoke cell stress responses. Cumulatively, the presence of redox signals affects the operation of cellular networks, from genome and proteome to other omes, leading to the integration of the redox signal. An additional mode of redox regulation is provided by the multifaceted interaction of various types of regulatory non-coding RNAs (such as microRNAs (miRNAs), long non-coding RNAs (lncRNAs) or circular RNAs (circRNAs)). The hormetic outcome of activation of various stress responses such as the oxidative distress response (ODR), unfolded protein response (UPR), heat shock response (HSR), integrated stress response (ISR) and the DNA damage response (DDR) results in the decision among adaptation, maintenance, repair and health or cell destruction, removal or death. Continuously operating feedback loops serve to maintain redox stability by iterative coordinated fluctuations and oscillations, including gene expression feedback loops or transcription–translation feedback loops, which then reinforce or desensitize the redox signal."
                - paragraph [ref=e179]:
                  - text: In addition to oxidant generation by various dehydrogenases, oxidases, oxidoreductases, oxygenases and electron transferring complexes, various types of cell stimuli, such as growth factors, nutrients and toxins, trigger the controlled production of waves of redox second messengers, notably of H
                  - subscript [ref=e180]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e181]: "2"
                  - text: ", •NO and H"
                  - subscript [ref=e182]: "2"
                  - text: S at low physiological concentrations (reviewed elsewhere
                  - superscript [ref=e183]:
                    - link "3" [ref=e184] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R3"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "9" [ref=e185] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R9"
                  - text: ). Networks of redox-regulated proteins within cellular compartments and between cells and organs then orchestrate and integrate the signals for the maintenance of life processes. The set of principles that underlie the organization of redox systems has been formulated as the ‘redox code’
                  - superscript [ref=e186]:
                    - link "22" [ref=e187] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R22"
                  - text: .
                  - link "Box 1" [ref=e188] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#BX1"
                  - text: "briefly presents this set of organizational principles as well as a few novel perspectives from currently active research. The paradigm of steady state relates to the maintenance of continuously ongoing metabolic flux: as life processes are characterized by non-equilibrium thermodynamics"
                  - superscript [ref=e189]:
                    - link "23" [ref=e190] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R23"
                  - text: ", maintaining steady state requires the constant monitoring and reprogramming of redox fluctuations, that is, the spatiotemporal changes in oxidant levels"
                  - superscript [ref=e191]:
                    - link "24" [ref=e192] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R24"
                  - text: . This is impressively exemplified even at a larger-scale structural level by the continuous reshaping of mitochondrial cristae — the invaginations formed by the folding of the inner mitochondrial membrane that are enriched in oxidant-producing dehydrogenases and electron transfer chain (ETC) complexes — at a timescale of seconds
                  - superscript [ref=e193]:
                    - link "25" [ref=e194] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R25"
                  - text: . The classical concept of
                  - emphasis [ref=e195]: homeostasis
                  - text: ", as elaborated by the pioneers in this field, Paracelsus, Claude Bernard, Walter Cannon and Ludwig von Bertalanffy, centres on constancy of the internal milieu"
                  - superscript [ref=e196]:
                    - link "26" [ref=e197] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R26"
                  - text: . However, biological systems are ‘homeodynamic’ in view of constantly ongoing metabolic flux because of their ability to dynamically self-organize
                  - superscript [ref=e198]:
                    - link "27" [ref=e199] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R27"
                  - text: . Thus, redox homeodynamics, rather than redox homeostasis, would be a more appropriate term to use.
                  - link "Figure 1" [ref=e200] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#F1"
                  - text: depicts the flow of redox information in cells, specifically the different physiological and environmental cues that converge on cells through redox switches and networks to induce responses and functional outcomes, including iterative feedback loops such as gene expression feedback loops
                  - superscript [ref=e201]:
                    - link "28" [ref=e202] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R28"
                  - text: . Understanding how cells respond to such stimuli is a challenging task
                  - superscript [ref=e203]:
                    - link "29" [ref=e204] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R29"
                  - text: ", which will require an even more detailed molecular insight of the processes involved. However, as discussed subsequently, recent advances in redox biology methods have revealed mechanisms of how cells integrate various redox regulatory pathways for intracellular and intercellular communication."
                - generic [ref=e205]:
                  - 'heading "Box 1. Principles underlying biological redox regulation: the redox code." [level=3] [ref=e206]'
                  - paragraph [ref=e207]:
                    - text: A set of fundamental principles of organization of biological redox reactions in living cells and organisms, termed redox code, was formulated in 2015 (ref.
                    - link "22" [ref=e208] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R22"
                    - text: ). Here, the four principles are presented briefly. As indicated in its initial formulation
                    - superscript [ref=e209]:
                      - link "22" [ref=e210] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R22"
                    - text: ", it is open to updates as redox research reveals new insights, documented by a recent extension to the plant world"
                    - superscript [ref=e211]:
                      - link "261" [ref=e212] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R261"
                    - text: . Current literature on relevant research is presented here in an exemplary fashion.
                  - paragraph [ref=e213]: "First principle: bioenergetics, catabolism and anabolism are organized through high-flux NADH and NADPH systems, respectively, operating at near equilibrium with central metabolic fuels."
                  - paragraph [ref=e214]:
                    - text: Redox regulation of NADH and NADPH systems occurs in various subcellular compartments
                    - superscript [ref=e215]:
                      - link "128" [ref=e216] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R128"
                    - text: . The content of these coenzyme systems in cells is controlled by regulatory enzymes in several ways. The enzyme NAD
                    - superscript [ref=e217]: +
                    - text: kinase (NADK) replenishes NADP
                    - superscript [ref=e218]: +
                    - text: from NAD
                    - superscript [ref=e219]: +
                    - text: (ref.
                    - link "262" [ref=e220] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R262"
                    - text: ), and the enzyme energy-linked nicotinamide nucleotide transhydrogenase (NNT) replenishes NADPH from NADH
                    - superscript [ref=e221]:
                      - link "263" [ref=e222] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R263"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "264" [ref=e223] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R264"
                    - text: ". NADPH has a dual function in redox regulation: it provides reducing equivalents for the redoxins, that is, the thioredoxin and glutaredoxin systems, as well as for glutathione disulfide reductase, whereas it generates oxidants by reducing molecular oxygen to superoxide in a reaction catalysed by NADPH oxidases (NOX). NAD"
                    - superscript [ref=e224]: +
                    - text: is a co-substrate for many enzymes involved in regulating metabolism, including the sirtuin family of protein deacetylases
                    - superscript [ref=e225]:
                      - link "265" [ref=e226] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R265"
                    - text: .
                  - paragraph [ref=e227]: "Second principle: macromolecular structure and activities are linked to NADH and NADPH systems through kinetically controlled sulfur switches in the redox proteome."
                  - paragraph [ref=e228]:
                    - text: Redox signalling by oxidants and electrophiles occurs predominantly through reversible post-translational protein cysteine modifications, which impact macromolecular structure and activity
                    - superscript [ref=e229]:
                      - link "13" [ref=e230] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R13"
                    - text: . Redox signalling by oxidants, foremost H
                    - subscript [ref=e231]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e232]: "2"
                    - text: ", proceeds through reversible molecular thiol redox switches. These are protein cysteinyl and methionyl residues, which are particularly suited owing to their reactivity, abundance and location for reversible signal transduction"
                    - superscript [ref=e233]:
                      - link "169" [ref=e234] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R169"
                    - text: . The cellular redox proteome describes the overall redox state in molecular terms, as exemplified by the oximouse data set
                    - superscript [ref=e235]:
                      - link "153" [ref=e236] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R153"
                    - text: ", accessible at ‘OxiMouse’."
                  - paragraph [ref=e237]:
                    - text: "Third principle: activation and deactivation cycles of H"
                    - subscript [ref=e238]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e239]: "2"
                    - text: production linked to NADH and NADPH systems support redox signalling and spatiotemporal sequential responses for differentiation and multicellular development.
                  - paragraph [ref=e240]:
                    - text: Endogenous and exogenous cues initiate oxidant generation at specific cellular locations. These signals serve as second messengers to reach target sites, predominantly by redox relay, for example, peroxiredoxins for H
                    - subscript [ref=e241]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e242]: "2"
                    - text: . For •NO, signalling occurs by diffusion to the target or by transnitrosylation through
                    - emphasis [ref=e243]: S
                    - text: "-nitrosoglutathione (GSNO)."
                  - paragraph [ref=e244]: "Fourth principle: redox networks, from microcompartments to subcellular and cellular organization, form an adaptative system to respond to the environment."
                  - paragraph [ref=e245]:
                    - text: Redox regulatory signals are highly integrated. Oxidant producers, antioxidant systems and redox-sensitive responders embedded in redox networks promote redox communication within cells and between cells. Peroxiporins permit the transport of H
                    - subscript [ref=e246]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e247]: "2"
                    - text: through membranes, which is important in maintaining gradients in the cellular H
                    - subscript [ref=e248]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e249]: "2"
                    - text: landscape. Alternatively, oxidants move between cells directly via gap junctions or indirectly through the extracellular space
                    - superscript [ref=e250]:
                      - link "136" [ref=e251] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R136"
                    - text: or by extracellular vesicles
                    - superscript [ref=e252]:
                      - link "74" [ref=e253] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R74"
                    - text: . Spatiotemporal redox control occurs at regulatory hubs that connect redox signalling with other stress response signalling pathways and hormones, as well as organelle-to-organelle and cell-to-cell signal transduction
                    - superscript [ref=e254]:
                      - link "16" [ref=e255] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R16"
                    - text: .
              - generic [ref=e256]:
                - heading "Redox eustress, oxidative and reductive distress" [level=2] [ref=e257]: Redox eustress, oxidative and reductive distress
                - paragraph [ref=e258]:
                  - text: The main direction of redox reactions in aerobic cell metabolism is towards oxidation, which is counterbalanced by reduction reactions using NADH and NADPH. The concept of oxidative stress (see refs.
                  - link "30" [ref=e259] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R30"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "31" [ref=e260] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R31"
                  - text: ) refers to the deviation from the physiological redox balance. Oxidative stress has been defined as ‘an imbalance between oxidants and antioxidants in favour of the oxidants, leading to a disruption of redox signalling and control and/or molecular damage’
                  - superscript [ref=e261]:
                    - link "30" [ref=e262] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R30"
                  - text: ". The focus on the damaging aspect prevailed in the field until relatively recently. However, it became clear that oxidative stress is two-sided: attention has shifted from the effects of supraphysiological concentrations of oxidants to the role of oxidants in the physiological concentration range. The perception of ongoing redox monitoring under physiological conditions, mentioned earlier, has led to the term eustress, Greek ‘eu’ for good stress, to denote the dynamic nature in redox regulation. This normal physiological range of deviations has also been called homeodynamic space"
                  - superscript [ref=e263]:
                    - link "32" [ref=e264] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R32"
                  - text: ", Goldilocks zone"
                  - superscript [ref=e265]:
                    - link "33" [ref=e266] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R33"
                  - text: ", or the golden mean (aurea mediocritas)"
                  - superscript [ref=e267]:
                    - link "34" [ref=e268] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R34"
                  - text: . H
                  - subscript [ref=e269]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e270]: "2"
                  - text: is a central redox signalling molecule in oxidative eustress, operating by two-electron oxidation of protein thiols
                  - superscript [ref=e271]:
                    - link "35" [ref=e272] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R35"
                  - text: . Redox signalling by a one-electron mechanism, that is, through free radicals, may also occur in eustress, but this requires further exploration
                  - superscript [ref=e273]:
                    - link "36" [ref=e274] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R36"
                  - text: .
                  - link "Figure 2" [ref=e275] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#F2"
                  - text: illustrates the homeodynamic range in which oxidants are sensed to activate beneficial oxidative eustress signals that are essential to maintain cell functions, adaptation and resilience in response to physiological cues such as exercise or changes in nutritional status. Although this Review focuses mainly on eustress in redox regulation, we will briefly mention the cellular responses to supraphysiological deviations from redox balance (that is, oxidative distress), which has multifaceted pathophysiological and medical consequences. Conversely, the overabundance of reductants such as antioxidants or NADPH triggers a stress response called reductive distress, which also results in cell dysfunction (
                  - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e276] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#F2"
                  - text: ).
                - figure [ref=e277]:
                  - heading "Fig. 2 |. Redox eustress and transition to oxidative and reductive distress." [level=3] [ref=e278]
                  - paragraph [ref=e279]:
                    - img "Fig. 2 |" [ref=e280]
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e282] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: figure/F2/
                  - paragraph [ref=e284]:
                    - text: The spectrum of oxidant exposures ranges from minimal (reductive distress) to physiological (redox eustress) to very high levels (oxidative distress). The hormetic range includes transition phases from physiological (blue) to non-physiological (red) exposure levels, that is, the hormetic dose–response, which results in the activation of redox-sensing transcription factors that promote cell adaptation. The borderline thresholds from physiological to subphysiological or supraphysiological levels are denoted as redox stress signalling thresholds (RSTs)
                    - superscript [ref=e285]:
                      - link "65" [ref=e286] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R65"
                    - text: . The hypoxia-inducible factor 1 (HIF-1) is activated predominantly when oxidant levels are low (towards the subphysiological side), whereas the nuclear factor-κB (NF-kB) comes into action towards the supraphysiological side, and the nuclear factor erythroid 2-related factor (NRF2) predominantly covers the physiological range. The bottom of the figure lists some of the major causes of redox eustress and of the two forms of redox distress with the resulting effects mentioned subsequently as detailed in the main text.
                - generic [ref=e287]:
                  - heading "Cellular responses to oxidative distress" [level=3] [ref=e288]
                  - paragraph [ref=e289]:
                    - text: The understanding of the cellular responses to redox stress resulted predominantly from studies on the effect of increasing oxidant concentrations on cell and organismal biochemistry and physiology. As noted in
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e290] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#F1"
                    - text: ", cells use various proteins, enzymes and transcriptional networks to respond to the increased exposure to oxidants, which disrupt eustress signals and can cause irreversible macromolecular damage. Response to redox stress is governed by redox-sensitive proteins that are modified by oxidants and in this modified form change their function"
                    - superscript [ref=e291]:
                      - link "37" [ref=e292] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R37"
                    - text: . One major group of redox-regulated proteins involved in counteracting oxidative distress are transcriptional regulators (
                    - link "Table 1" [ref=e293] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#T1"
                    - text: ), which elicit adaptive responses to physiological increases in cell oxidations
                    - superscript [ref=e294]:
                      - link "38" [ref=e295] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R38"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "39" [ref=e296] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R39"
                    - text: . A recent study examined how changes in transcription factor activation upon exposure to oxidants are temporally coordinated
                    - superscript [ref=e297]:
                      - link "40" [ref=e298] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R40"
                    - text: ", with metabolic and transcriptomic responses being dependent on cell type and oxidant concentration"
                    - superscript [ref=e299]:
                      - link "41" [ref=e300] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R41"
                    - text: . A particularly well-characterized sensor for physiological changes in oxidant levels is the ubiquitously expressed nuclear factor erythroid 2-related factor (NRF2) and its inhibitor Kelch-like ECH-associated protein 1 (KEAP1)
                    - superscript [ref=e301]:
                      - link "42" [ref=e302] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R42"
                    - text: . KEAP1 is an oxidant and electrophile sensor that associates with Cullin 3 (Cul3)-based ubiquitin ligase to target NRF2 for proteasomal degradation when the oxidant level is low
                    - superscript [ref=e303]:
                      - link "43" [ref=e304] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R43"
                    - text: . An increase in oxidants triggers the oxidation of specific cysteines in KEAP1, which causes NRF2 release and diffusion to the nucleus, leading to the expression of a battery of antioxidant genes required for GSH biosynthesis and the elimination of H
                    - subscript [ref=e305]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e306]: "2"
                    - text: (for example, GSH peroxidase)
                    - superscript [ref=e307]:
                      - link "44" [ref=e308] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R44"
                    - text: . This NRF2-dependent upregulation of gene expression forms the basis of the dose–response mechanism of hormesis
                    - superscript [ref=e309]:
                      - link "45" [ref=e310] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R45"
                    - text: (
                    - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e311] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#F2"
                    - text: ). The general principle of hormesis
                    - superscript [ref=e312]:
                      - link "46" [ref=e313] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R46"
                    - text: has wide-ranging importance as a fundamental mechanism of resilience, as documented, for example, in neuroprotection
                    - superscript [ref=e314]:
                      - link "47" [ref=e315] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R47"
                    - text: . NRF2 also activates the NRF2-induced secretory phenotype genes, an immune surveillance system, which clears cells that have been damaged beyond the ability for repair
                    - superscript [ref=e316]:
                      - link "48" [ref=e317] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R48"
                    - text: . Other redox-regulated transcription factors include the hypoxia inducible factor 1 (HIF-1α) and the heat shock factor 1 (HSF-1). Both hypoxic and heat shock conditions lead to the perinuclear accumulation of mitochondria and cause increased ROS production in the nucleus
                    - superscript [ref=e318]:
                      - link "49" [ref=e319] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R49"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "50" [ref=e320] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R50"
                    - text: . While HIF-1α appears to be stabilized by the oxidant-mediated inactivation of prolyl hydroxylase and induces a number of genes involved in glycolysis and GSH biosynthesis, HSF-1 activity is sensitive to oxidant-mediated cysteine sulfenylation and induces the expression of molecular chaperones and other components of the proteostasis network
                    - superscript [ref=e321]:
                      - link "51" [ref=e322] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R51"
                    - text: .

                  - paragraph [ref=e486]:
                    - text: In addition to being directly or indirectly activated by oxidants, several of the redox-relevant transcription factors are modulated by either short (<200 nt, sncRNA) or long (>200 nt, lncRNA) ncRNAs, whose expression levels are induced by H
                    - subscript [ref=e487]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e488]: "2"
                    - text: (ref.
                    - link "52" [ref=e489] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R52"
                    - text: ). For instance, lncRNAs such as LINC00239 and the microRNA (miRNA)-200a trigger KEAP1 mRNA degradation, which increases NRF2 stability
                    - superscript [ref=e490]:
                      - link "53" [ref=e491] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R53"
                    - text: . The antisense lncRNA HIFAL enhances HIF-1-mediated transcription of glycolytic genes
                    - superscript [ref=e492]:
                      - link "54" [ref=e493] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R54"
                    - text: ", and the lncRNA HEAT is a transcriptional brake for HSF-1, used to nullify the heat shock response when it is no longer needed"
                    - superscript [ref=e494]:
                      - link "55" [ref=e495] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R55"
                    - text: . Together, these ncRNAs serve as a superimposed signal that works in tandem with oxidants to modulate critical redox-sensitive transcription factors that activate cell stress responses.
                - generic [ref=e496]:
                  - heading "Cellular response to reductive distress" [level=3] [ref=e497]
                  - paragraph [ref=e498]:
                    - text: Reductive distress, which can be perilous for the cell as well, can occur in response to chronic NRF2 activation. For instance, it can be elicited by increased expression of aldehyde dehydrogenase 3, an enzyme that produces NADH
                    - superscript [ref=e499]:
                      - link "56" [ref=e500] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R56"
                    - text: . To counteract reductive distress, designated sensor proteins exist that, when reduced, activates oxidant-producing pathways
                    - superscript [ref=e501]:
                      - link "57" [ref=e502] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R57"
                    - text: (
                    - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e503] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#F2"
                    - text: ). One such sensor protein is the folliculin-interacting protein 1 (FNIP1), which, in its oxidized form, serves as a gatekeeper for mitochondrial H
                    - subscript [ref=e504]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e505]: "2"
                    - text: generation by dampening Krebs cycle metabolism and oxidative phosphorylation
                    - superscript [ref=e506]:
                      - link "57" [ref=e507] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R57"
                    - text: . Reduction of this protein increases the flux of electrons through the ETC, thus increasing oxidant production. In addition, reductive distress can be mitigated by the induction of lactate dehydrogenase
                    - superscript [ref=e508]:
                      - link "56" [ref=e509] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R56"
                    - text: . Thus, reductive stress sensing is a vital response that is required to counter the chronic overstimulation of oxidant response systems such as NRF2.
                - generic [ref=e510]:
                  - heading "Integration of cellular stress responses" [level=3] [ref=e511]
                  - paragraph [ref=e512]:
                    - text: A critical research field regards the question of when and how the various cellular stress responses are activated following redox distress
                    - superscript [ref=e513]:
                      - link "58" [ref=e514] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R58"
                    - text: . The oxidative distress response is interconnected and fine-tuned with other types of stress responses, such as the integrated stress response
                    - superscript [ref=e515]:
                      - link "59" [ref=e516] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R59"
                    - text: ", unfolded protein response (UPR)"
                    - superscript [ref=e517]:
                      - link "60" [ref=e518] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R60"
                    - text: ", heat shock response (HSR)"
                    - superscript [ref=e519]:
                      - link "51" [ref=e520] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R51"
                    - text: ", DNA damage response (DDR)"
                    - superscript [ref=e521]:
                      - link "61" [ref=e522] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R61"
                    - text: and others. NRF2, NF-κB, HIF-1 and HSF-1 are interconnected with the integrated stress response, UPR, HSR and DDR to integrate redox sensing into these stress responses
                    - superscript [ref=e523]:
                      - link "62" [ref=e524] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R62"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "63" [ref=e525] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R63"
                    - text: . Here, it is important to point out that the major systems of redox stress response, that is, NRF2, NF-κB, HIF-1 and so on (
                    - link "Table 1" [ref=e526] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#T1"
                    - text: and
                    - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e527] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#F2"
                    - text: ), operate at an idling low basal rate rather than serving as an on–off switch, tuning gene expression responses to meet the redox needs of the cell. This allows for swift responses to any sudden increases in oxidants. What defines the tipping point, that is, the transition from eustress to distress? The answer is likely context-specific and cell-specific. Experimentally, this can be monitored and further explored at the single-cell level
                    - superscript [ref=e528]:
                      - link "64" [ref=e529] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R64"
                    - text: . Calculation of a redox stress signalling threshold (RST) (
                    - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e530] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#F2"
                    - text: ) has been approached experimentally in the nematode
                    - emphasis [ref=e531]: Caenorhabditis elegans
                    - text: . Although exposure to the superoxide (O
                    - subscript [ref=e532]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e533]: •−
                    - text: ) generator paraquat caused an increase in lifespan at low concentrations, higher levels of exposure resulted in the shortening of lifespan
                    - superscript [ref=e534]:
                      - link "65" [ref=e535] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R65"
                    - text: . These experiments delineated the oxidant concentration range used by
                    - emphasis [ref=e536]: C. elegans
                    - text: to activate redox regulatory pathways and the threshold at which concentration oxidative distress occurs. Using cell culture, it has been found that low doses of H
                    - subscript [ref=e537]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e538]: "2"
                    - text: trigger the UPR and HSR, which is required to maintain protein stability in response to mild heat preconditioning
                    - superscript [ref=e539]:
                      - link "66" [ref=e540] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R66"
                    - text: . Similarly, DDR is activated by mild oxidant stress to precondition cells to an overt distress challenge
                    - superscript [ref=e541]:
                      - link "67" [ref=e542] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R67"
                    - text: .
              - generic [ref=e543]:
                - heading "Intracellular and intercellular redox signalling" [level=2] [ref=e544]: Intracellular and intercellular redox signalling
                - paragraph [ref=e545]:
                  - text: An active topic in redox research concerns the question as to how locally produced oxidants and global changes in redox states are communicated within and between cells. Many growth factor receptors
                  - superscript [ref=e546]:
                    - link "68" [ref=e547] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R68"
                  - text: ", integrins"
                  - superscript [ref=e548]:
                    - link "69" [ref=e549] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R69"
                  - text: ", cytokines and other signalling modes (see the section ‘"
                  - link "Redox adaptation to the environment" [ref=e550] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#S24"
                  - text: ’) initiate a redox cascade directly at the plasma membrane that results in redox communication within cells and between different cellular locations
                  - superscript [ref=e551]:
                    - link "70" [ref=e552] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R70"
                  - text: . This section presents the current knowledge on redox communication.
                - generic [ref=e553]:
                  - heading "Intracellular redox communication" [level=3] [ref=e554]
                  - paragraph [ref=e555]: The various subcellular cytological spaces each have their specific redox microenvironment, and within them there is considerable heterogeneity and fluctuation. To enable discussion on intracellular redox communication, we will first present the key redox features of individual cellular locations.
                  - generic [ref=e556]:
                    - heading "Plasma membrane." [level=4] [ref=e557]
                    - paragraph [ref=e558]:
                      - text: Plasma membrane-embedded NADPH oxidases (NOX) are activated by signal-mediated phosphorylation to produce superoxide anion radicals, which subsequently dismutate to H
                      - subscript [ref=e559]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e560]: "2"
                      - text: in a reaction catalysed by extracellular superoxide dismutase (SOD3) (
                      - link "Fig. 3" [ref=e561] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#F3"
                      - text: ). This NOX–SOD3 extracellular redox tandem is crucial for redox signalling, for example, from macrophages to other cell types
                      - superscript [ref=e562]:
                        - link "71" [ref=e563] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R71"
                      - text: . The extracellular H
                      - subscript [ref=e564]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e565]: "2"
                      - text: diffuses into cells through specialized aquaporins (AQPs), the peroxiporins
                      - superscript [ref=e566]:
                        - link "72" [ref=e567] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R72"
                      - text: ", or by the formation of redox-active endosomes"
                      - superscript [ref=e568]:
                        - link "73" [ref=e569] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R73"
                      - text: ", which were named redoxosomes"
                      - superscript [ref=e570]:
                        - link "74" [ref=e571] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R74"
                      - text: ", opening the way for subsequent intracellular redox signalling. Release of redoxosomes from the cell to the extracellular space allows them to contribute to redox signal transduction between cells"
                      - superscript [ref=e572]:
                        - link "75" [ref=e573] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R75"
                      - text: .
                    - figure [ref=e574]:
                      - heading "Fig. 3 |. Schematic overview of redox communication in and between cells." [level=5] [ref=e575]
                      - paragraph [ref=e576]:
                        - img "Fig. 3 |" [ref=e577]
                      - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e579] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: figure/F3/
                      - paragraph [ref=e581]:
                        - text: This simplified scheme focuses on H
                        - subscript [ref=e582]: "2"
                        - text: O
                        - subscript [ref=e583]: "2"
                        - text: as a major cellular signalling oxidant. Plasma membrane-located NADPH oxidase (NOX) generates O
                        - subscript [ref=e584]: "2"
                        - superscript [ref=e585]: •−
                        - text: extracellularly, which is then dismutated to H
                        - subscript [ref=e586]: "2"
                        - text: O
                        - subscript [ref=e587]: "2"
                        - text: by superoxide dismutase (SOD3). Entry of H
                        - subscript [ref=e588]: "2"
                        - text: O
                        - subscript [ref=e589]: "2"
                        - text: into cells occurs either by transfer through peroxiporins, which are specialized aquaporins (AQPs), or by endocytosis to form redox-active endosomes
                        - superscript [ref=e590]:
                          - link "73" [ref=e591] [cursor=pointer]:
                            - /url: "#R73"
                        - text: ", which have been named ‘redoxosomes’"
                        - superscript [ref=e592]:
                          - link "75" [ref=e593] [cursor=pointer]:
                            - /url: "#R75"
                        - text: ", formed predominantly at caveolae"
                        - superscript [ref=e594]:
                          - link "258" [ref=e595] [cursor=pointer]:
                            - /url: "#R258"
                        - text: . Intracellularly, the organelles such as mitochondria, peroxisomes and the endoplasmic reticulum (ER) as well as around 40 oxidases produce H
                        - subscript [ref=e596]: "2"
                        - text: O
                        - subscript [ref=e597]: "2"
                        - text: for signalling (see ref.
                        - link "9" [ref=e598] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R9"
                        - text: ). The three organelles are in close contact, facilitated by tethering of their membranes at membrane contact sites (MCSs)
                        - superscript [ref=e599]:
                          - link "118" [ref=e600] [cursor=pointer]:
                            - /url: "#R118"
                        - text: . Intercellular redox communication occurs by gap junctional communication through connexons, formed from two connexin hemichannels between directly adjacent cells, or by AQPs or endocytosis of extracellular vesicles (EVs), the redoxosomes mentioned earlier. The desmosomes and tight junctions serve to connect cells to one another, bringing the adjacent cell receiving the redox signal near the cell producing the H
                        - subscript [ref=e601]: "2"
                        - text: O
                        - subscript [ref=e602]: "2"
                        - text: messenger. The protein desmin has a single cysteine (Cys333), which upon oxidation causes desmosome disruption
                        - superscript [ref=e603]:
                          - link "259" [ref=e604] [cursor=pointer]:
                            - /url: "#R259"
                        - text: . For distant cells, H
                        - subscript [ref=e605]: "2"
                        - text: O
                        - subscript [ref=e606]: "2"
                        - text: signalling can occur by endocytosis of redoxosomes or through AQPs. It should be noted that other modes of redox communication exist, for example, the release of redox-active enzymes for paracrine signalling between cells
                        - superscript [ref=e607]:
                          - link "141" [ref=e608] [cursor=pointer]:
                            - /url: "#R141"
                        - text: ", and the release of nitric oxide from nitric oxide synthase. The full potential of the redoxosome and other EVs in cell-to-cell redox signalling needs to be explored. EGFR, epidermal growth factor receptor."
                  - generic [ref=e609]:
                    - heading "Cytoskeleton." [level=4] [ref=e610]
                    - paragraph [ref=e611]:
                      - text: The actin cytoskeleton is a major target for redox regulation
                      - superscript [ref=e612]:
                        - link "76" [ref=e613] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R76"
                        - text: ","
                        - link "77" [ref=e614] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R77"
                      - text: ". In β-actin, oxidation of Cys374 decreases the rate of filamentous (F-)actin polymerization and induces actomyosin disassembly, which contributes to a contraction of the cytoskeleton during cell migration. It is noteworthy that there is a functional relationship between the cytoskeleton and the NRF2–KEAP1 antioxidant system: KEAP1 binds to actin filaments through its double glycine repeat domain, which was found to be required for its NRF2 suppressing activity"
                      - superscript [ref=e615]:
                        - link "78" [ref=e616] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R78"
                      - text: . Microtubules as components of the cytoskeleton are also subject to oxidative modification
                      - superscript [ref=e617]:
                        - link "79" [ref=e618] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R79"
                      - text: . Among the various filament systems of the cytoskeleton, the cellular proteins comprising the group of intermediate filaments, including keratins, vimentin and lamin, have redox-responsive roles in stress resilience
                      - superscript [ref=e619]:
                        - link "80" [ref=e620] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R80"
                      - text: .
                  - generic [ref=e621]:
                    - heading "Peroxisomes." [level=4] [ref=e622]
                    - paragraph [ref=e623]:
                      - text: The perception of the role of peroxisomes in redox regulation (reviewed elsewhere
                      - superscript [ref=e624]:
                        - link "81" [ref=e625] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R81"
                      - text: ) has expanded considerably
                      - superscript [ref=e626]:
                        - link "82" [ref=e627] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R82"
                        - text: ","
                        - link "83" [ref=e628] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R83"
                      - text: . Peroxisomally generated H
                      - subscript [ref=e629]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e630]: "2"
                      - text: was shown to target more than 400 proteins, which are located within peroxisomes and, importantly, also outside the organelle in the cytosol and in mitochondria. H
                      - subscript [ref=e631]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e632]: "2"
                      - text: generated in peroxisomes affects target proteins by modulating their sulfenylation profiles, which contributes to interorganelle redox signalling
                      - superscript [ref=e633]:
                        - link "84" [ref=e634] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R84"
                      - text: . Furthermore, the peroxisome counteracts oxidative challenge by suppressing the import of catalase into the peroxisome
                      - superscript [ref=e635]:
                        - link "85" [ref=e636] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R85"
                      - text: ", thereby increasing the cellular resistance to oxidative distress by enhancing the capacity of extraperoxisomal catalase"
                      - superscript [ref=e637]:
                        - link "86" [ref=e638] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R86"
                      - text: . Peroxisomal redox signalling modulates cellular immunometabolism by several mechanisms, including through NF-κB activation
                      - superscript [ref=e639]:
                        - link "87" [ref=e640] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R87"
                      - text: .
                  - generic [ref=e641]:
                    - heading "Endoplasmic reticulum." [level=4] [ref=e642]
                    - paragraph [ref=e643]:
                      - text: The endoplasmic reticulum is considered to be a master regulator of membrane trafficking and organelle function and dynamics (discussed subsequently)
                      - superscript [ref=e644]:
                        - link "88" [ref=e645] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R88"
                      - text: . Among its many functions, the endoplasmic reticulum has a major role in oxidative protein folding for secretion. Disulfide bonds are formed by the combined action of protein disulfide isomerases and endoplasmic oxidoreductin-1, leading to the generation of one H
                      - subscript [ref=e646]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e647]: "2"
                      - text: per disulfide bond
                      - superscript [ref=e648]:
                        - link "89" [ref=e649] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R89"
                      - text: . Maintenance of redox balance in the endoplasmic reticulum is important for counteracting proteotoxic stress and its consequences
                      - superscript [ref=e650]:
                        - link "90" [ref=e651] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R90"
                      - text: .
                  - generic [ref=e652]:
                    - heading "Mitochondria." [level=4] [ref=e653]
                    - paragraph [ref=e654]:
                      - text: Mitochondria represent a cornerstone in redox regulation
                      - superscript [ref=e655]:
                        - link "91" [ref=e656] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R91"
                        - text: ","
                        - link "92" [ref=e657] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R92"
                      - text: ", ranging from cristae dynamics to interorganellar crosstalk"
                      - superscript [ref=e658]:
                        - link "93" [ref=e659] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R93"
                      - text: with a multitude of redox-related functions beyond bioenergetics
                      - superscript [ref=e660]:
                        - link "94" [ref=e661] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R94"
                        - text: –
                        - link "96" [ref=e662] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R96"
                      - text: . How oxidants are produced by mitochondria has long been an active research topic
                      - superscript [ref=e663]:
                        - link "97" [ref=e664] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R97"
                      - text: ", with the current focus on the dynamics of H"
                      - subscript [ref=e665]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e666]: "2"
                      - text: flux between the endoplasmic reticulum and mitochondria at membrane contact points called endoplasmic reticulum–mitochondria microdomains
                      - superscript [ref=e667]:
                        - link "98" [ref=e668] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R98"
                        - text: ","
                        - link "99" [ref=e669] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R99"
                      - text: . Exhaustive literature exists on the role of mitochondria, particularly the ETC, in redox regulation
                      - superscript [ref=e670]:
                        - link "100" [ref=e671] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R100"
                      - text: . Two prototypical examples of mitochondrial processes that are redox-regulated concern thermogenesis
                      - superscript [ref=e672]:
                        - link "101" [ref=e673] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R101"
                      - text: and the circadian rhythm
                      - superscript [ref=e674]:
                        - link "102" [ref=e675] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R102"
                      - text: ", which are not presented here in detail."
                  - generic [ref=e676]:
                    - heading "Nucleus." [level=4] [ref=e677]
                    - paragraph [ref=e678]:
                      - text: Although the nucleus is only a minor site of oxidant generation, it has a role in multiple aspects of cellular redox regulation. Nucleoredoxin, a member of the thioredoxin family located in the nucleus
                      - superscript [ref=e679]:
                        - link "103" [ref=e680] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R103"
                      - text: ", is a key regulator of cellular redox control. It shuttles between the nucleus and the cytosol and interacts with several proteins in a redox-sensitive manner"
                      - superscript [ref=e681]:
                        - link "104" [ref=e682] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R104"
                      - text: . A further indirect contribution by DNA to redox regulation is oxidatively generated base modifications in DNA. In particular, 7,8-dihydro-8-oxoguanine (8-oxoG), in conjunction with the repair glycosylase OGG1, serves as an epigenetic regulatory mark
                      - superscript [ref=e683]:
                        - link "105" [ref=e684] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R105"
                      - text: . OGG1 binds 8-oxoG and facilitates activator protein recruitment
                      - superscript [ref=e685]:
                        - link "106" [ref=e686] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R106"
                      - text: . The biological significance of 8-oxoG in redox regulation expands also into epitranscriptional modifications, for example, covalent modification of RNA
                      - superscript [ref=e687]:
                        - link "107" [ref=e688] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R107"
                      - text: . Chromatin remodelling factors such as lysine-specific histone demethylase 1A, which associates with other chromatin regulator proteins such as histone deacetylases, have also been implicated in generating H
                      - subscript [ref=e689]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e690]: "2"
                      - text: for proximal redox regulation of DNA repair proteins and histone demethylation
                      - superscript [ref=e691]:
                        - link "108" [ref=e692] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R108"
                      - text: .
                    - paragraph [ref=e693]:
                      - text: As mentioned earlier, ncRNAs provide a complex layer of differential gene expression by impinging on redox control mechanisms
                      - superscript [ref=e694]:
                        - link "109" [ref=e695] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R109"
                      - text: . Although an oxidant challenge alters the expression of mRNAs
                      - superscript [ref=e696]:
                        - link "52" [ref=e697] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R52"
                      - text: and lncRNAs
                      - superscript [ref=e698]:
                        - link "110" [ref=e699] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R110"
                      - text: ", miRNAs"
                      - superscript [ref=e700]:
                        - link "111" [ref=e701] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R111"
                      - text: and circular RNAs
                      - superscript [ref=e702]:
                        - link "112" [ref=e703] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R112"
                      - text: alter the expression of enzymes, which carry out redox regulation. For example, miR-23 functionally acts in skeletal muscle differentiation by modulating the expression of thioredoxin reductase-1 (ref.
                      - link "113" [ref=e704] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R113"
                      - text: ). The p53 pathway is centrally involved in the ncRNA response to oxidative distress
                      - superscript [ref=e705]:
                        - link "114" [ref=e706] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R114"
                      - text: .
                  - generic [ref=e707]:
                    - heading "Redox interplay between cell organelles." [level=4] [ref=e708]
                    - paragraph [ref=e709]:
                      - text: Redox signalling pathways between subcellular organelles emerge as a central means of control. Elucidation of a realm of novel interorganellar contacts and the structural and functional identification of microdomains and nanodomains
                      - superscript [ref=e710]:
                        - link "115" [ref=e711] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R115"
                        - text: ","
                        - link "116" [ref=e712] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R116"
                      - text: and of molecular crowding
                      - superscript [ref=e713]:
                        - link "117" [ref=e714] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R117"
                      - text: introduced a new dimension of analysis. The general layout of subcellular redox organization (see refs.
                      - link "3" [ref=e715] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R3"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "9" [ref=e716] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R9"
                      - text: ) has been brought to a new level by studies of the organelles in situ within the intact cell and organ, as opposed to studies with preparations of isolated organelles, which are devoid of the surrounding cellular microenvironment.
                    - paragraph [ref=e717]:
                      - text: The endoplasmic reticulum–mitochondria–peroxisome contacts have been called ‘redox triangle’, with all three organelles being in proximity with each other through membrane contact sites. They generate and transport oxidants through aquaporins and unidentified channels into the cytoplasm
                      - superscript [ref=e718]:
                        - link "118" [ref=e719] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R118"
                      - text: . By including the physically close plasma membrane as an important contributor to redox regulation, one could even speak of a ‘redox quadrangle’. In addition, the organelles also form redox interactions with lysosomes and lipid droplets
                      - superscript [ref=e720]:
                        - link "119" [ref=e721] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R119"
                      - text: . Mitochondria-associated membranes between endoplasmic reticulum and mitochondria have a role in lipid and Ca
                      - superscript [ref=e722]: 2+
                      - text: transfer and modulate redox signalling between these organelles
                      - superscript [ref=e723]:
                        - link "120" [ref=e724] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R120"
                      - text: . The peroxiporin AQP11 permits efficient transport of H
                      - subscript [ref=e725]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e726]: "2"
                      - text: across the endoplasmic reticulum membrane
                      - superscript [ref=e727]:
                        - link "121" [ref=e728] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R121"
                      - text: and facilitates transfer of mitochondrial H
                      - subscript [ref=e729]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e730]: "2"
                      - text: to the endoplasmic reticulum
                      - superscript [ref=e731]:
                        - link "122" [ref=e732] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R122"
                      - text: . Plasma membrane aquaporins interact with the endoplasmic reticulum at endoplasmic reticulum–plasma membrane contact sites
                      - superscript [ref=e733]:
                        - link "123" [ref=e734] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R123"
                      - text: . Different tissues also express unique AQP isoforms, which may influence the transport of H
                      - subscript [ref=e735]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e736]: "2"
                      - text: through these endoplasmic reticulum–plasma membrane contact sites
                      - superscript [ref=e737]:
                        - link "124" [ref=e738] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R124"
                      - text: . Mitochondria–plasma membrane contacts serve to efficiently exchange metabolites and ions and generate signalling platforms enriched with various proteins. Mitochondria can even be translocated between cells through tunnelling nanotubes
                      - superscript [ref=e739]:
                        - link "125" [ref=e740] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R125"
                      - text: ", which might promote the exchange of H"
                      - subscript [ref=e741]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e742]: "2"
                      - text: for redox signalling propagation.
                - generic [ref=e743]:
                  - heading "Intercellular redox communication" [level=3] [ref=e744]
                  - paragraph [ref=e745]:
                    - text: Redox communication between cells is an essential feature of organ and systemic physiology (
                    - link "Fig. 3" [ref=e746] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#F3"
                    - text: ). It occurs via several modalities, which include direct and indirect diffusion from cell to cell, gap junctional communication between cells through connexons
                    - superscript [ref=e747]:
                      - link "126" [ref=e748] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R126"
                    - text: and extracellular vesicles. A prototypical example for intercellular diffusion of redox metabolites is the equilibration of the cytosolic NADH/NAD
                    - superscript [ref=e749]: +
                    - text: redox potential between cells and organs through the lactate–pyruvate redox couple
                    - superscript [ref=e750]:
                      - link "127" [ref=e751] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R127"
                    - text: ", a larger research field reviewed in reference"
                    - superscript [ref=e752]:
                      - link "128" [ref=e753] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R128"
                    - text: . Gap junctional communication occurs through connexins, transmembrane proteins which form a channel, called the connexon, between the two hemichannel connexins in membranes of adjacent cells
                    - superscript [ref=e754]:
                      - link "129" [ref=e755] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R129"
                    - text: . When open, these channels permit rapid intercellular exchange of small molecules (up to about 1,000 Da), thus connecting the cytoplasm of two neighbouring cells. In mutual interplay between connexins and redox signalling, there is redox regulation of the open–closed state of the channel by post-translational modification
                    - superscript [ref=e756]:
                      - link "130" [ref=e757] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R130"
                    - text: .
                  - paragraph [ref=e758]:
                    - text: Cell-to-cell communication by extracellular vesicles is another modality of short-distance and long-distance interactions
                    - superscript [ref=e759]:
                      - link "131" [ref=e760] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R131"
                    - text: . Extracellular vesicles, including exosomes and ectosomes (microvesicles), are produced at the plasma membrane or in the endolysosomal system. They have been recognized as key components of intercellular communication that carry and deliver selective cargo in context-specific regulatory processes
                    - superscript [ref=e761]:
                      - link "132" [ref=e762] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R132"
                    - text: . Understanding the function of extracellular vesicles in intercellular redox communication is emerging rapidly
                    - superscript [ref=e763]:
                      - link "133" [ref=e764] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R133"
                    - text: . Specialized redox-active endosomes called redoxosomes are formed downstream of various cell surface receptors when activated by the binding of a ligand (
                    - link "Fig. 3" [ref=e765] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#F3"
                    - text: ). These redoxosomes, mentioned earlier
                    - superscript [ref=e766]:
                      - link "74" [ref=e767] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R74"
                    - text: ", may also become processed to be released from cells as exosomes, carrying with them redox-active components"
                    - superscript [ref=e768]:
                      - link "134" [ref=e769] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R134"
                    - text: and aquaporin channels
                    - superscript [ref=e770]:
                      - link "135" [ref=e771] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R135"
                    - text: . An illustrative example of redox signalling between cells is provided by the regeneration of injured axons upon transfer of macrophage-derived redox-active extracellular vesicles to neuronal endosomes
                    - superscript [ref=e772]:
                      - link "134" [ref=e773] [cursor=pointer]:
