                        - /url: "#R134"
                    - text: . In this case, exosomes containing NOX2 fuse with injured axons. NOX2 then generates oxidants that deactivate the phosphatase PTEN, prolonging the induction of the PI3K–AKT pathway for axonal regeneration in injured neurons
                    - superscript [ref=e774]:
                      - link "134" [ref=e775] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R134"
                    - text: .
                  - paragraph [ref=e776]:
                    - text: The pericellular redox microenvironment exhibits considerable diversity. Propagation of redox signalling between cells in a paracrine fashion as a response to stress has been visualized as ‘ROS wave’
                    - superscript [ref=e777]:
                      - link "16" [ref=e778] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R16"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "136" [ref=e779] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R136"
                    - text: . The communication between neighbouring cells can elicit adaptive responses for enhanced resilience to environmental stressors. In mammalian cells, the external leaflet of the plasma membrane facing towards the extracellular space is in contact with the extracellular matrix
                    - superscript [ref=e780]:
                      - link "137" [ref=e781] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R137"
                    - text: . Tissue-specific cellular proteoglycan profiles of the extracellular matrix contribute to regulating redox signalling pathways, and there is a connection between functional changes of glycans and the redox state, called the glyco–redox interplay
                    - superscript [ref=e782]:
                      - link "138" [ref=e783] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R138"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "139" [ref=e784] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R139"
                    - text: . Owing to the action of glycosyltransferases and glycosidases, properties of redox-active enzymes, for example, superoxide dismutases, can be modified. The mutual relationship between redox changes and protein glycosylation, predominantly O-GlcNAcylation, constitutes an important crosstalk of signalling pathways for stress responses
                    - superscript [ref=e785]:
                      - link "140" [ref=e786] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R140"
                    - text: . Emerging evidence of pericellular release of thioredoxin, peroxiredoxins, protein disulfide isomerases and sulfhydryl oxidases points to their role as mediators of extracellular redox signalling (for example, in the modulation of immune cell function)
                    - superscript [ref=e787]:
                      - link "141" [ref=e788] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R141"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "142" [ref=e789] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R142"
                    - text: .
                  - paragraph [ref=e790]:
                    - text: These observations on the pericellular environment open new perspectives of extracellular redox control in body fluids
                    - superscript [ref=e791]:
                      - link "143" [ref=e792] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R143"
                    - text: . The extracellular space in mammals is composed of the (larger) interstitial space and the vascular bed. The concentration of H
                    - subscript [ref=e793]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e794]: "2"
                    - text: in blood plasma has been reported to be 1–5 μM (ref.
                    - link "144" [ref=e795] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R144"
                    - text: ), and probing the surface of the cellular plasma membrane with a biosensor using surface-enhanced Raman spectroscopy revealed unevenly distributed patches with H
                    - subscript [ref=e796]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e797]: "2"
                    - text: concentrations between 1 μM and 12 μM, whereas the intracellular H
                    - subscript [ref=e798]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e799]: "2"
                    - text: concentration was 5 nM (ref.
                    - link "145" [ref=e800] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R145"
                    - text: ).
              - generic [ref=e801]:
                - heading "The redox-regulated proteome" [level=2] [ref=e802]: The redox-regulated proteome
                - paragraph [ref=e803]:
                  - text: Redox eustress depends on the production of oxidants and reductants in response to cell stimuli (
                  - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e804] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#F1"
                  - text: ). The redox regulation of cell function is predominantly carried out not only by powerful redox-active enzymes but also by various biomolecules such as ncRNAs and molecules derived from DNA, lipids and carbohydrates and low-molecular mass compounds, such as nutritionally supplied cofactors and micronutrients as well as vitamins and ‘biofactors’ (tocopherols, carotenoids, ascorbate and polyphenols). Eustress signals are propagated through the redox proteome, which is a collective term for components of the proteome that undergo reversible or irreversible redox reactions
                  - superscript [ref=e805]:
                    - link "146" [ref=e806] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R146"
                  - text: . The redox proteome relies on the ability of cysteine, methionine, selenocysteine and selenomethionine to undergo reversible redox reactions. Other amino acids, such as tryptophan, tyrosine, arginine and the protein backbone, can also be oxidized
                  - superscript [ref=e807]:
                    - link "147" [ref=e808] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R147"
                  - text: . The redox proteome senses, propagates and amplifies the redox signal. Signal transduction in redox regulation utilizes the small-molecule reactive species mentioned earlier (ROS, RNS, RSS and others), which are summarized as the reactive species interactome
                  - superscript [ref=e809]:
                    - link "148" [ref=e810] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R148"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "149" [ref=e811] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R149"
                  - text: (
                  - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e812] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#F1"
                  - text: ). Operationally, in redox research, the global term redox proteome is often used in a more restricted sense to denote the cysteine redox proteome
                  - superscript [ref=e813]:
                    - link "150" [ref=e814] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R150"
                  - text: .
                - generic [ref=e815]:
                  - heading "Protein cysteines in redox regulation" [level=3] [ref=e816]
                  - paragraph [ref=e817]:
                    - text: Oxidative post-translational modifications of protein cysteines (
                    - link "Box 2" [ref=e818] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#BX2"
                    - text: ) have a major regulatory role at the cellular, tissue-wide and organismic levels
                    - superscript [ref=e819]:
                      - link "151" [ref=e820] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R151"
                    - text: . Fuelled by the development of sophisticated quantitative redox proteomic techniques that can differentiate between various thiol modifications
                    - superscript [ref=e821]:
                      - link "152" [ref=e822] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R152"
                    - text: as well as improvements in the detection of low-abundance proteins by mass spectrometry
                    - superscript [ref=e823]:
                      - link "153" [ref=e824] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R153"
                    - text: ", proteins that undergo reversible cysteine modification can now be studied at the single-cell level. A comprehensive assessment of cell-specific and tissue-specific protein cysteine oxidation in vivo was conducted in young and aged mice, generating a data set called Oximouse which lists the oxidation state of approximately 171,000 cysteine residues across 10 tissues"
                    - superscript [ref=e825]:
                      - link "153" [ref=e826] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R153"
                    - text: . About 34,000 cysteine sites in approximately 9,400 proteins were found to undergo reversible cysteine oxidation, underscoring that redox signals are a ubiquitous phenomenon in mammals. This compendium, which is accessible through an interactive web resource (‘
                    - link "External, opens in a new tab. Oximouse" [ref=e827] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: https://oximouse.hms.harvard.edu/
                    - text: ’), enables validation of distinct cysteine oxidation networks that govern the unique physiological and metabolic profile of each tissue. It is noteworthy that caution should be exercised when interpreting redox proteomic data because of the extraordinary sensitivity of the current methods, which also detect cysteine residues that are not targeted by redox signals. Therefore, follow-up studies that interrogate the cell function of candidate cysteines identified using redox proteomics should be conducted
                    - superscript [ref=e828]:
                      - link "154" [ref=e829] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R154"
                    - text: . As techniques in redox proteomics are still becoming more refined and targeted, valuable further insight into the cell effects of reversible cysteine switches will become available
                    - superscript [ref=e830]:
                      - link "155" [ref=e831] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R155"
                    - text: .
                  - generic [ref=e832]:
                    - heading "Box 2. Cysteine oxidative post-translational modifications." [level=4] [ref=e833]
                    - figure [ref=e834]:
                      - paragraph [ref=e835]:
                        - img "graphic file with name nihms-2061548-f0005.jpg" [ref=e836]
                      - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e838] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: figure/F5/
                    - paragraph [ref=e839]:
                      - text: Cysteine thiols in proteins are the most prominent targets for modification by oxidants and electrophilic agents. Many of the oxidative post-translational modifications of cysteines shown here are reversible, making them suitable to serve as redox switches to control the structure and/or function of redox-regulated proteins
                      - superscript [ref=e840]:
                        - link "266" [ref=e841] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R266"
                      - text: . Oxidation of cysteine thiolate by H
                      - subscript [ref=e842]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e843]: "2"
                      - text: results in S-sulfenylation. However, most protein thiols react only slowly with H
                      - subscript [ref=e844]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e845]: "2"
                      - text: ", limiting the impact of direct thiol oxidation. By contrast, peroxiredoxins (Prx, right) contain the most reactive cysteines by far, their second-order reaction constant with H"
                      - subscript [ref=e846]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e847]: "2"
                      - text: being approximately 10
                      - superscript [ref=e848]: "7"
                      - text: mol
                      - superscript [ref=e849]: −1
                      - text: s
                      - superscript [ref=e850]: −1
                      - text: ", and hence about five to six orders of magnitude higher than those for most other reactive protein cysteines (left)"
                      - superscript [ref=e851]:
                        - link "267" [ref=e852] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R267"
                      - text: . Therefore, peroxiredoxins are the predominant target for S-sulfenylation. Peroxiredoxin then oxidizes a thiolate in a target protein, which is known as a redox relay mechanism
                      - superscript [ref=e853]:
                        - link "268" [ref=e854] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R268"
                      - text: . Sulfenate formation deactivates peroxiredoxins, which can be rapidly reversed by GSH peroxidases and thioredoxins. However, sulfenates can also be further oxidized by H
                      - subscript [ref=e855]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e856]: "2"
                      - text: to sulfinates
                      - superscript [ref=e857]:
                        - link "269" [ref=e858] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R269"
                      - text: . Sulfinates can be reversed by sulfiredoxins. Further oxidation of sulfinates by H
                      - subscript [ref=e859]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e860]: "2"
                      - text: yields sulfonates, which occurs during oxidative distress. Alternatively, the sulfenate can react with thiol-containing compounds, forming mixed disulfides with GSH (R-SSG) (S-glutathionylation)
                      - superscript [ref=e861]:
                        - link "168" [ref=e862] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R168"
                      - text: ", cysteine (R-SS-Cys), homocysteine (R-SS-HCys)"
                      - superscript [ref=e863]:
                        - link "270" [ref=e864] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R270"
                      - text: or coenzyme A (R-SSCoA)
                      - superscript [ref=e865]:
                        - link "271" [ref=e866] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R271"
                      - text: ", or engages in intramolecular or intermolecular disulfide bonds upon reaction with nearby protein cysteines (R-SSR). Reaction of cysteine thiols with RNS such as nitric oxide (NO) results in"
                      - emphasis [ref=e867]: S
                      - text: "-nitrosothiol (R-SNO)"
                      - superscript [ref=e868]:
                        - link "272" [ref=e869] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R272"
                      - text: ", whereas its interaction with hydrogen sulfide (H"
                      - subscript [ref=e870]: "2"
                      - text: S) causes persulfidation (R-SSH)
                      - superscript [ref=e871]:
                        - link "270" [ref=e872] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R270"
                        - text: ","
                        - link "273" [ref=e873] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R273"
                      - text: . Persulfidated cysteines can be reduced back to native thiols
                      - superscript [ref=e874]:
                        - link "224" [ref=e875] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R224"
                      - text: . Other oxidative cysteine modifications include cysteine–lysine (NOS)
                      - superscript [ref=e876]:
                        - link "274" [ref=e877] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R274"
                      - text: and cysteine–lysine–cysteine (SONOS)
                      - superscript [ref=e878]:
                        - link "275" [ref=e879] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R275"
                      - text: redox bridges, which are protein crosslinks that occur among nitrogen (N), oxygen (O) and sulfur (S). Reduction of most of these oxidative post-translational modifications is enzymatically catalysed by members of the thioredoxin and glutaredoxin families, which use NADPH as their ultimate electron donor.
                  - paragraph [ref=e880]:
                    - text: Reaction-based fluorogenic probes for detecting protein cysteinyl oxidation (CysOx) enable researchers to follow protein S-sulfenylation sites, the first step in thiol oxidation processes, and to identify regulatory cysteines in living cells
                    - superscript [ref=e881]:
                      - link "156" [ref=e882] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R156"
                    - text: . Moreover, the redox proteomics analysis can be combined with chemogenetic tools, such as d-amino acid oxidase, which allows for the controlled production of H
                    - subscript [ref=e883]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e884]: "2"
                    - text: in a subset of cells or tissues upon d-amino acid supplementation
                    - superscript [ref=e885]:
                      - link "157" [ref=e886] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R157"
                    - text: . By combining d-amino acid oxidase with a H
                    - subscript [ref=e887]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e888]: "2"
                    - text: "-sensing module such as HyPer, in vivo H"
                    - subscript [ref=e889]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e890]: "2"
                    - text: synthesis can be simultaneously stimulated and analysed
                    - superscript [ref=e891]:
                      - link "158" [ref=e892] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R158"
                    - text: . This approach has provided unique new insights into the interplay between redox-sensitive proteins and altered metabolism
                    - superscript [ref=e893]:
                      - link "159" [ref=e894] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R159"
                    - text: ", as also illustrated by a study on the effect of H"
                    - subscript [ref=e895]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e896]: "2"
                    - text: on cardiac contractility
                    - superscript [ref=e897]:
                      - link "160" [ref=e898] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R160"
                    - text: . The HyPer probe was also used to identify H
                    - subscript [ref=e899]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e900]: "2"
                    - text: microdomains in yeast cells by fusing the probe to the C terminus of individual yeast proteins, creating a screenable yeast strain library
                    - superscript [ref=e901]:
                      - link "161" [ref=e902] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R161"
                    - text: .
                  - paragraph [ref=e903]:
                    - text: The combined analysis of the redox proteomes of bacteria, flies, worms, mice and human tissues revealed that redox-sensitive proteins are embedded in nearly every metabolic pathway and physiological process of the cell
                    - superscript [ref=e904]:
                      - link "162" [ref=e905] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R162"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "163" [ref=e906] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R163"
                    - text: . The specificity and sensitivity of protein cysteines for oxidation results from local electrostatic modulation of the surrounding physicochemical microenvironment and is heavily influenced by neighbouring charged side chains such as Arg or Lys
                    - superscript [ref=e907]:
                      - link "153" [ref=e908] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R153"
                    - text: . Cysteine-coordinating metal centres, particularly zinc centres, are also targets for oxidative cysteine modification
                    - superscript [ref=e909]:
                      - link "164" [ref=e910] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R164"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "165" [ref=e911] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R165"
                    - text: . Proteins such as protein phosphatases can be found in different types of oxidative modifications, depending on their specific location in the cell
                    - superscript [ref=e912]:
                      - link "166" [ref=e913] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R166"
                    - text: .
                  - paragraph [ref=e914]:
                    - text: The mechanisms by which thiol oxidation affects protein function are highly varied and typically require detailed case-by-case biochemical and structural analysis; prominent examples are given in
                    - link "Table 1" [ref=e915] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#T1"
                    - text: . Many active-site cysteines can become targets for oxidative modifications, resulting in a physiologically relevant functional redox switch. One prototypical example is glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate dehydrogenase, whose active-site cysteine can be modified by several types of oxidative post-translational modifications, eliciting post-translational-modification-specific cell responses
                    - superscript [ref=e916]:
                      - link "167" [ref=e917] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R167"
                    - text: . Oxidative deactivation of glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate dehydrogenase in response to a moderate increase in cell oxidants re-routes glucose metabolism towards the pentose phosphate pathway, which increases NADPH generation, to maintain cell redox state. The activity of many other enzymes is also decreased by reversible cysteine oxidation, resulting in the reconfiguration of metabolism for cell signalling
                    - superscript [ref=e918]:
                      - link "168" [ref=e919] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R168"
                    - text: .
                  - paragraph [ref=e920]:
                    - text: Thiol oxidation of cysteines that are not located at the active site can also lead to functional outcomes, ranging from protein degradation and protein re-localization to the acquisition of new protein functions. These are triggered by conformational changes in proteins that range from more subtle alterations in the hydrogen-bonding network surrounding the affected thiols
                    - superscript [ref=e921]:
                      - link "169" [ref=e922] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R169"
                    - text: to major structural rearrangements that can initiate local unfolding events
                    - superscript [ref=e923]:
                      - link "170" [ref=e924] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R170"
                    - text: ", changes in oligomerization state"
                    - superscript [ref=e925]:
                      - link "171" [ref=e926] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R171"
                    - text: or solubility
                    - superscript [ref=e927]:
                      - link "172" [ref=e928] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R172"
                    - text: . These conformational changes depend on the intramolecular interaction network of the affected cysteine thiols and are mediated by either specific thiol oxidation products, such as S-glutathionylation or disulfide bond formation, or the simple loss of a stabilizing thiol group
                    - superscript [ref=e929]:
                      - link "173" [ref=e930] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R173"
                    - text: . The yeast ATPase GET3, for instance, combines some of these features. Upon the initial oxidation of two critical cysteines, GET3 locally unfolds, oligomerizes and concomitantly switches from an ATP-dependent targeting factor to an effective ATP-independent chaperone, which protects proteins against the toxic effects of protein oxidation
                    - superscript [ref=e931]:
                      - link "173" [ref=e932] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R173"
                    - text: .
                - generic [ref=e933]:
                  - heading "Protein methionine in redox regulation" [level=3] [ref=e934]
                  - paragraph [ref=e935]:
                    - text: Methionine (Met) can be oxidized to methionine sulfoxide (MetO) and then reduced back to Met through stereospecific methionine sulfoxide reductases (Msrs)
                    - superscript [ref=e936]:
                      - link "174" [ref=e937] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R174"
                    - text: . Oxidation of Met generates a chiral centre at the sulfur atom with the two epimers, R-MetO and S-MetO
                    - superscript [ref=e938]:
                      - link "174" [ref=e939] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R174"
                    - text: ". Correspondingly, there are two Msr isozymes that reduce these centres and promote specificity for regulation: MsrA (R-MetO) and MsrB (S-MetO)"
                    - superscript [ref=e940]:
                      - link "174" [ref=e941] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R174"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "175" [ref=e942] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R175"
                    - text: . Oxidation of protein Met residues can be catalysed by monooxygenases of the molecule interacting with CasL (MICAL) family in conjunction with MsrB1, a process controlled by the polymerization of monomeric actin
                    - superscript [ref=e943]:
                      - link "176" [ref=e944] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R176"
                    - text: . Several other functional targets of the balance of Met and oxidized Met (MetO) in proteins by MetO–Msr pairs have been identified, ranging from calcium regulation, the ubiquitination system, to transcription factors (for example, FOXO)
                    - superscript [ref=e945]:
                      - link "174" [ref=e946] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R174"
                    - text: .
                - generic [ref=e947]:
                  - heading "Protein selenocysteine and selenomethionine in redox regulation and protection" [level=3] [ref=e948]
                  - paragraph [ref=e949]:
                    - text: Selenocysteine, the 21st proteinogenic amino acid, is contained in 25 human proteins, many of which function in redox regulation
                    - superscript [ref=e950]:
                      - link "177" [ref=e951] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R177"
                    - text: . Selenocysteine exhibits higher nucleophilicity and a lower pK
                    - subscript [ref=e952]: a
                    - text: than Cys, resulting in higher redox reactivity
                    - superscript [ref=e953]:
                      - link "177" [ref=e954] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R177"
                    - text: . Thioredoxin reductases and GSH peroxidases are among the most prominent members of the selenoproteome. Their role in redox regulation has been studied widely (for a recent review, see ref.
                    - link "178" [ref=e955] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R178"
                    - text: ). Selenomethionine, which is synthesized in some plants and fungi, enters mammalian systems through the food chain and incorporates directly into proteins in place of methionine. It reacts with oxidants through a radical-mediated mechanism, whose reaction rate is approximately 10-fold higher compared with methionine
                    - superscript [ref=e956]:
                      - link "179" [ref=e957] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R179"
                    - text: . The oxidation product selenomethionine oxide can be rapidly and efficiently reduced back to selenomethionine by GSH, establishing a highly effective defence line against ROS and RNS
                    - superscript [ref=e958]:
                      - link "180" [ref=e959] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R180"
                    - text: .
                - generic [ref=e960]:
                  - heading "Protein tyrosine in redox regulation" [level=3] [ref=e961]
                  - paragraph [ref=e962]:
                    - text: Oxidation of tyrosines results in the formation of either stable covalently linked
                    - emphasis [ref=e963]: o,o
                    - text: ′-di-tyrosines or 3,4-dihydroxy-l-phenylalanine (l-dopa)
                    - superscript [ref=e964]:
                      - link "181" [ref=e965] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R181"
                    - text: . Recent proteomic studies in
                    - emphasis [ref=e966]: Escherichia coli
                    - text: revealed more than 70 different proteins, which are sensitive to di-tyrosine formation upon exposure to exogenous or endogenous stress
                    - superscript [ref=e967]:
                      - link "182" [ref=e968] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R182"
                    - text: . However, given that reducing systems have not (yet) been identified, it is possible that tyrosine oxidation serves as a result of oxidative damage rather than to redox-regulating protein activity
                    - superscript [ref=e969]:
                      - link "182" [ref=e970] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R182"
                    - text: . Nitration of protein tyrosine by peroxynitrite to form 3-nitro-tyrosine is indirectly related to redox regulation through the role of the superoxide radical, which reacts with •NO to form peroxynitrite in a diffusion-controlled reaction
                    - superscript [ref=e971]:
                      - link "183" [ref=e972] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R183"
                    - text: . Thus, the nitroproteome has a role in cell signalling
                    - superscript [ref=e973]:
                      - link "10" [ref=e974] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R10"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "184" [ref=e975] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R184"
                    - text: .
                - generic [ref=e976]:
                  - heading "Molecular and organismal redox heterogeneity" [level=3] [ref=e977]
                  - paragraph [ref=e978]:
                    - text: The cellular distribution of biomolecules that can adopt different redox states is not uniform, and proteins with oxidizable cysteine thiols can be present in a number of distinct protein forms, termed ‘oxiforms’
                    - superscript [ref=e979]:
                      - link "185" [ref=e980] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R185"
                    - text: . For example, a protein with three cysteines can adopt eight functionally distinct oxidation states (if only two cysteine redox states are considered), which provides the pattern of individual protein molecules over the space of a cell with a distinct ‘redox signature’. Oxiform heterogeneity can increase further by several orders of magnitude if one considers that cells generate many types of oxidants
                    - superscript [ref=e981]:
                      - link "186" [ref=e982] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R186"
                    - text: . This oxiform pattern significantly enhances the protein redox heterogeneity within cells. As there are many more than only two forms of redox-active protein cysteines (see the
                    - link "figure" [ref=e983] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#F5"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Box 2" [ref=e984] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#BX2"
                    - text: ), the number of potential oxiforms of a protein at a given cell condition is amplified enormously.
                  - paragraph [ref=e985]:
                    - text: Redox heterogeneity also exists at the level of individual cells. With the recent development of genetically encoded in vivo redox sensor proteins, such as the H
                    - subscript [ref=e986]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e987]: "2"
                    - text: "-sensing HyPer"
                    - superscript [ref=e988]:
                      - link "187" [ref=e989] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R187"
                    - text: or the GSH redox-state sensor Grx-roGFP
                    - superscript [ref=e990]:
                      - link "188" [ref=e991] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R188"
                    - text: ", it has become obvious that even highly synchronized populations of clonal cells"
                    - superscript [ref=e992]:
                      - link "189" [ref=e993] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R189"
                    - text: or whole isogenic organisms such as
                    - emphasis [ref=e994]: C. elegans
                    - superscript [ref=e995]:
                      - link "190" [ref=e996] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R190"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "191" [ref=e997] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R191"
                    - text: show pronounced interindividual differences in the redox state. Analysis of subpopulations of clonal cells or
                    - emphasis [ref=e998]: C. elegans
                    - text: with distinct redox states uncovered redox-sensitive processes that would go unnoticed in whole population studies. These studies revealed that intrinsic redox heterogeneity early in life contributes to interindividual differences in the epigenetic landscape, expression patterns and ultimately the lifespan of organisms
                    - superscript [ref=e999]:
                      - link "192" [ref=e1000] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R192"
                    - text: .
              - generic [ref=e1001]:
                - heading "Redox regulation of cellular processes" [level=2] [ref=e1002]: Redox regulation of cellular processes
                - paragraph [ref=e1003]:
                  - text: The cellular redox environment contributes to the regulation of most cellular processes through redox-regulated proteins. Some prominent examples of such processes are depicted in
                  - link "Fig. 4" [ref=e1004] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#F4"
                  - text: . and summarized here. The redox environment fine-tunes metabolic pathways and modulates the metabolome, which affects the activity of enzymes such as the NAD
                  - superscript [ref=e1005]: +
                  - text: "-dependent histone deacetylases (that is, the sirtuins), and in response alters the epigenome"
                  - superscript [ref=e1006]:
                    - link "193" [ref=e1007] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R193"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "194" [ref=e1008] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R194"
                  - text: . Redox-regulated histone modifier enzymes affect gene expression in response to changing oxidant levels, promoting potentially long-lasting and even transgenerational memory effects
                  - superscript [ref=e1009]:
                    - link "192" [ref=e1010] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R192"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "195" [ref=e1011] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R195"
                  - text: . Oxidants can upregulate transcription factor biosynthesis or alter their stability, for example, of NRF2 (discussed earlier) at the level of transcription, mRNA stability and translation. Transcription factor activity is modified by post-translational modifications, which are instigated by redox-regulated kinases and phosphatases
                  - superscript [ref=e1012]:
                    - link "39" [ref=e1013] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R39"
                  - text: . Nearly every aspect of protein synthesis, ranging from mRNA stability and translation initiation to ribosome structure and transfer RNA dynamics, involves redox-sensitive components, charged with adjusting global protein translation rates to the redox conditions of the cell
                  - superscript [ref=e1014]:
                    - link "3" [ref=e1015] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R3"
                  - text: . Physiological levels of oxidants also regulate the activity of kinases, phosphatases and transcription factors to induce proliferation, differentiation and migration
                  - superscript [ref=e1016]:
                    - link "11" [ref=e1017] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R11"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "196" [ref=e1018] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R196"
                    - text: –
                    - link "198" [ref=e1019] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R198"
                  - text: . Finally, regulated cell death (RCD), that is, pathways such as apoptosis, engages redox-sensitive proteins to control the decision whether cells will live or die
                  - superscript [ref=e1020]:
                    - link "199" [ref=e1021] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R199"
                  - text: . Although their oxidation typically induces apoptosis
                  - superscript [ref=e1022]:
                    - link "200" [ref=e1023] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R200"
                  - text: ", modification of the active-site cysteine in executioner caspases – the proteases ultimately responsible for apoptotic cell death – is used to protect cells against unwanted activation of the irreversible apoptotic RCD programme"
                  - superscript [ref=e1024]:
                    - link "199" [ref=e1025] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R199"
                  - text: (
                  - link "Fig. 4f" [ref=e1026] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#F4"
                  - text: ). For instance, constitutive persulfidation (and hence inactivation) of pro-caspases 3 and 9 occurs in resting HeLa cells and is counteracted by thioredoxin-mediated de-persulfidation
                  - superscript [ref=e1027]:
                    - link "201" [ref=e1028] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R201"
                  - text: . The types of redox modifications and the mechanism by which pro-apoptotic signals activate redoxins and consequently caspases appear cell-type-specific and context-specific
                  - superscript [ref=e1029]:
                    - link "199" [ref=e1030] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R199"
                  - text: . Ferroptosis, a non-apoptotic form of RCD triggered by Fe
                  - superscript [ref=e1031]: 2+
                  - text: "-mediated peroxidation of unsaturated fatty acids"
                  - superscript [ref=e1032]:
                    - link "202" [ref=e1033] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R202"
                  - text: ", is effectively blocked by GSH peroxidase GPX4, which uses selenocysteine-based catalysis to reduce lipid hydroperoxides"
                  - superscript [ref=e1034]:
                    - link "203" [ref=e1035] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R203"
                  - text: . Loss of thioredoxin also contributes to ferroptosis, likely through a combination of oxidant accumulation and its effects on the GSH–glutaredoxin system
                  - superscript [ref=e1036]:
                    - link "204" [ref=e1037] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R204"
                  - text: . Treatment with ferroptocide, a small molecule that covalently modifies the active-site cysteines in thioredoxin, is highly effective in eliciting ferroptosis in cancer cells
                  - superscript [ref=e1038]:
                    - link "204" [ref=e1039] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R204"
                  - text: . Other redox-sensitive players engaged in RCDs include p62 involved in parthanatos, an RCD pathway primarily regulated by oxidant-mediated DNA damage
                  - superscript [ref=e1040]:
                    - link "205" [ref=e1041] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R205"
                  - text: ", the redox-sensitive KEAP1 (ref."
                  - link "206" [ref=e1042] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R206"
                  - text: ) involved in oxeiptosis, a ROS-dependent, caspase-independent RCD and the nucleotide-binding domain-like receptor protein 3 involved in pyroptosis
                  - superscript [ref=e1043]:
                    - link "207" [ref=e1044] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R207"
                  - text: ", an inflammatory cell death programme."
                - figure [ref=e1045]:
                  - heading "Fig. 4 |. Major cellular processes under redox regulation." [level=3] [ref=e1046]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1047]:
                    - img "Fig. 4 |" [ref=e1048]
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1050] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: figure/F4/
                  - paragraph [ref=e1052]:
                    - strong [ref=e1053]: a
                    - text: ", Metabolism: as a prototypical example for redox control of metabolic pathways, glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate dehydrogenase (GAPDH), a central enzyme of glycolysis, contains a redox-sensitive cysteine at the active site. Its oxidative modification causes loss of the dehydrogenase function (GAPDH"
                    - superscript [ref=e1054]: ox
                    - text: ) and blocks glycolysis. Glucose is shunted to the pentose phosphate pathway to form NADPH, which is needed to restore the reduced glutathione (GSH) pool. GAPDH
                    - superscript [ref=e1055]: ox
                    - text: can also shuttle into the nucleus, where it oxidatively modifies and inactivates the NAD-dependent protein deacetylase sirtuin-1 (SIRT1). Failure to deacetylate p53 can lead to apoptotic cell death.
                    - strong [ref=e1056]: b
                    - text: ", Epigenetics: the epigenetic landscape is fine-tuned by the prevailing redox environment of the cell. Illustrated here are two redox-sensitive histone modifiers: the histone-lysine"
                    - emphasis [ref=e1057]: "N"
                    - text: "-methyltransferase 2A (MLL1, also known as KMT2A), a member of the COMPASS complex, contains a redox-sensitive zinc centre, whose oxidation leads to a decrease in global H3K4me3 levels. Oxidation of SIRT1 results in the inactivation of its deacetylase function and hence in the accumulation of acetylated lysines on histones. This contributes to alterations in gene expression."
                    - strong [ref=e1058]: c
                    - text: ", Gene expression: oxidants and electrophiles directly activate select transcription factors, resulting in changes in gene expression ("
                    - link "Table 1" [ref=e1059] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#T1"
                    - text: ). A prominent example is the Kelch-like ECH-associated protein 1 (KEAP1), which binds the nuclear factor erythroid 2-related factor (NRF2) and targets it for degradation under non-stress conditions. Oxidation of redox-sensitive cysteines in KEAP1 leads to the release of NRF2, which subsequently relocalizes to the nucleus, heterodimerizes with a member of the small MAF (sMAF) family of proteins and activates genes encoding proteins of the antioxidant defence systems.
                    - strong [ref=e1060]: d
                    - text: ", Protein translation, folding and degradation: these processes are either directly or indirectly controlled through redox-regulated proteins. Oxidative distress causes a global restriction of protein synthesis by causing the phosphorylation of initiation factors (for example, eIF2α), changes in transfer RNA dynamics and ribosome function. The ATP-dependent folding chaperone (that is, foldase) heat shock protein 70 (HSP70) or the protein targeting factor GET3 serves as examples of oxidative stress-activated chaperones. Oxidative modification of key thiols leads to their conversion into ATP-independent holding chaperones (that is, holdases), which bind unfolding proteins and prevent their aggregation. This stress-specific activation compensates for the oxidative-stress-mediated decrease in cellular ATP levels and the concomitant functional downregulation of ATP-dependent chaperones. Protein ubiquitination and 26S proteasome function are inhibited by redox-mediated changes, whereas 20S proteasome activity increases to degrade oxidatively damaged proteins."
                    - strong [ref=e1061]: e
                    - text: ", Proliferation, differentiation and migration: many kinases and transcription factors that induce proliferation and differentiation are under redox control. Activation occurs either through the direct oxidation of cysteine switches or indirectly through inhibition of upstream inhibitors of the pathway. Activation of phosphatidyl inositol-3-kinase (PI3K) or extracellular signal-regulated kinases (ERK) is extended through the oxidative deactivation of redox-sensitive upstream phosphatases such as phosphatase and tensin homologue (PTEN) and protein tyrosine phosphatase 1B (PTP1B, not shown). Nuclear factor κ-light-chain-enhancer (NF-κB) is activated directly through cysteine oxidation and indirectly through inhibition of NF-κB inhibitors. Similarly, the stress-activated mitogen-activated protein kinase (MAPK) p38 is both indirectly and directly activated by the presence of oxidants."
                    - strong [ref=e1062]: f
                    - text: ", Regulated cell death (RCD) modes: parthanatos is an RCD pathway primarily regulated by oxidant-mediated DNA damage. Overactivation of poly[ADP-ribose] polymerase 1 (PARP1) overproduces polyA-ribose (PAR), which causes the release of the apoptosis-inducing factor from mitochondria causing cell death"
                    - superscript [ref=e1063]:
                      - link "205" [ref=e1064] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R205"
                    - text: . Ferroptosis, which is a non-apoptotic form of RCD, is triggered by Fe
                    - superscript [ref=e1065]: 2+
                    - text: "-mediated peroxidation of unsaturated fatty acids"
                    - superscript [ref=e1066]:
                      - link "202" [ref=e1067] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R202"
                    - text: . Necroptosis is mediated by the tumour necrosis factor (TNF), which activates a multiprotein complex called the necrosome. One redox-sensitive player of the necrosome is receptor-interacting serine/threonine-protein kinase 1 (RIPK1), which, upon oxidation, undergoes autophosphorylation, interacts with RIPK3 and mixed lineage kinase domain-like protein (MLKL) and forms an active necrosome
                    - superscript [ref=e1068]:
                      - link "260" [ref=e1069] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R260"
                    - text: . Apoptosis is mediated by a proteolytic cascade of (pro) caspases, which in their activated form degrade cellular components and cause cell death. Oxidative activation of the apoptotic regulator BAX or oxidative inactivation of the anti-apoptotic factor BFL1 (also known as BCL2A1) triggers apoptosis
                    - superscript [ref=e1070]:
                      - link "200" [ref=e1071] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R200"
                    - text: . Pyroptosis is an inflammatory form of cell death, involving the multiprotein complex inflammasome, whose member caspase 1 activates the pore complex gasdermin D. Inflammasomes containing the nucleotide-binding domain (NOD)-like receptor protein 3 (NLRP3) as their sensor module appear to be especially sensitive to oxidants
                    - superscript [ref=e1072]:
                      - link "207" [ref=e1073] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R207"
                    - text: . Oxeiptosis is an RCD pathway directly controlled by the redox-sensitive player KEAP1 (ref.
                    - link "206" [ref=e1074] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R206"
                    - text: ). Upon oxidation of KEAP1, the mitochondrial serine–threonine phosphatase PGAM5 is released and subsequently dephosphorylates and activates the death factor, apoptosis-inducing factor mitochondrial 1 (AIFM1).
              - generic [ref=e1075]:
                - heading "Redox adaptation to the environment" [level=2] [ref=e1076]: Redox adaptation to the environment
                - paragraph [ref=e1077]:
                  - text: Throughout the course of life, organisms depend on interactions with the external environment. The exposome
                  - superscript [ref=e1078]:
                    - link "19" [ref=e1079] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R19"
                  - text: denotes the lifelong pattern of exposure to various biotic and abiotic agents and includes components that are essential, foremost nutrition and oxygen supply. There are numerous chemical and physical components of the exposome, such as carcinogens, air pollution, noise, ionizing and solar radiation, as well as infections and lifestyle factors. In humans and other animals, a major location of exposomal interaction is on the epithelial–environmental interface, which encompasses the gastrointestinal and pulmonary spaces, the skin and the eye. These locations are home to a wealth of microorganisms, collectively called the microbiome. Nutritional, chemical and physical exposures have beneficial as well as potentially dangerous effects, depending on dose and spatiotemporal application
                  - superscript [ref=e1080]:
                    - link "208" [ref=e1081] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R208"
                  - text: . Monitoring the exposomal cues and integrating them into response and memory functions is the essential role of the various stress responses (see the discussion mentioned earlier).
                  - link "Box 3" [ref=e1082] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#BX3"
                  - text: directs attention to the exposome with emphasis on redox regulation and the role of the aryl hydrocarbon receptor (AHR), which is an ‘exposome receptor’
                  - superscript [ref=e1083]:
                    - link "209" [ref=e1084] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R209"
                  - text: . The interface of the exposome with the proteome, genome and epigenome involves a redox network that integrates the input signals into an adaptive cellular response output
                  - superscript [ref=e1085]:
                    - link "210" [ref=e1086] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R210"
                  - text: (
                  - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1087] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#F1"
                  - text: ). The complex role of AHR signalling in multiple life processes is beginning to be elucidated, for example, in the ageing process
                  - superscript [ref=e1088]:
                    - link "211" [ref=e1089] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R211"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "212" [ref=e1090] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R212"
                  - text: ", and in the redox regulation of immune cell metabolism"
                  - superscript [ref=e1091]:
                    - link "213" [ref=e1092] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R213"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "214" [ref=e1093] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R214"
                  - text: . Memory effects of redox changes have been shown to modulate immune responses
                  - superscript [ref=e1094]:
                    - link "215" [ref=e1095] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R215"
                  - text: .
                - generic [ref=e1096]:
                  - heading "Box 3. Exogenous exposure to oxidants." [level=3] [ref=e1097]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1098]:
                    - text: The profile of lifelong exposure to various cues and influences, termed ‘exposome’
                    - superscript [ref=e1099]:
                      - link "19" [ref=e1100] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#R19"
                    - text: ", includes not only essential and beneficial but also potentially detrimental components relevant to redox regulation. These include, foremost, nutrition and oxygen supply, and there are chemical, physical, infection and lifestyle exposomal components. Redox signalling networks serve as the interface between the exposome and the induction of changes in cell behaviour."
                  - generic [ref=e1101]:
                    - heading "Nutritional exposure." [level=4] [ref=e1102]
                    - paragraph [ref=e1103]:
                      - text: A fundamental role of nutrition is the tissue-specific and cell-specific provision of building blocks for tools of redox regulation
                      - superscript [ref=e1104]:
                        - link "276" [ref=e1105] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R276"
                      - text: . This includes the micronutrients and minerals for redox enzymes and low-molecular-mass redox-active biofactors such as vitamins and phytochemicals (carotenoids, flavonoids and so on). Macronutrients provide substrate hydrogen for NADPH and FADH
                      - subscript [ref=e1106]: "2"
                      - text: to fuel bioenergetics. Potentially toxic dietary components can be metabolized and detoxified by redox processes, notably monooxygenases (cytochromes P450), dioxygenases and other oxidases.
                  - generic [ref=e1107]:
                    - heading "Exposure to chemicals." [level=4] [ref=e1108]
                    - paragraph [ref=e1109]:
                      - text: Non-nutritional chemicals constitute the vast group of environmental agents, pollutants in particular, which are the focus of toxicology. Oxidative distress and inflammation are among the essential features of environmental insults
                      - superscript [ref=e1110]:
                        - link "208" [ref=e1111] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R208"
                      - text: . The aryl hydrocarbon receptor (AHR) serves to initiate redox-related responses to a wide variety of chemical exposures (‘exposome receptor’)
                      - superscript [ref=e1112]:
                        - link "209" [ref=e1113] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R209"
                      - text: . AHR is a ligand-activated transcription factor sensing exposomal cues
                      - superscript [ref=e1114]:
                        - link "277" [ref=e1115] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R277"
                      - text: ", which activates complex transcriptional responses for diverse pathways"
                      - superscript [ref=e1116]:
                        - link "216" [ref=e1117] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R216"
                      - text: ", with regulators and co-regulators to fine-tune the responses"
                      - superscript [ref=e1118]:
                        - link "278" [ref=e1119] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R278"
                      - text: . AHR ligands such as dioxin-like compounds interact with the extracellular domain of the epidermal growth factor receptor (EGFR), which impinges on its function in redox regulation
                      - superscript [ref=e1120]:
                        - link "279" [ref=e1121] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R279"
                      - text: . A workflow to biomonitor the human environmental exposome is available
                      - superscript [ref=e1122]:
                        - link "280" [ref=e1123] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R280"
                      - text: .
                  - generic [ref=e1124]:
                    - heading "Physical exposures." [level=4] [ref=e1125]
                    - paragraph [ref=e1126]:
                      - text: Detrimental redox reactions through supraphysiological exposure to solar radiation and other forms of radiations are of concern. These include ionizing and electromagnetic radiation, heat, noise and mechanical challenge, for which there are powerful stress responses. For instance, noise-induced cardiovascular and neuronal dysregulation is mediated by redox processes
                      - superscript [ref=e1127]:
                        - link "281" [ref=e1128] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R281"
                        - text: ","
                        - link "282" [ref=e1129] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R282"
                      - text: . Skin ageing can result from oxidative challenge by UV irradiation or air pollution
                      - superscript [ref=e1130]:
                        - link "283" [ref=e1131] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R283"
                      - text: .
                  - generic [ref=e1132]:
                    - heading "Infection and immune responses." [level=4] [ref=e1133]
                    - paragraph [ref=e1134]:
                      - text: Bacterial and viral infection is part of the external challenges to the organism, and redox regulation has a role in defence mechanisms
                      - superscript [ref=e1135]:
                        - link "247" [ref=e1136] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R247"
                        - text: ","
                        - link "284" [ref=e1137] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R284"
                      - text: . For instance, oxidants are pivotal for immune protection in the gastrointestinal tract
                      - superscript [ref=e1138]:
                        - link "285" [ref=e1139] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R285"
                        - text: ","
                        - link "286" [ref=e1140] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R286"
                      - text: .
                  - generic [ref=e1141]:
                    - heading "Lifestyle factors." [level=4] [ref=e1142]
                    - paragraph [ref=e1143]:
                      - text: Among various behavioural modes included in the exposome, physical exercise has redox activity connection by increasing the production of H
                      - subscript [ref=e1144]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e1145]: "2"
                      - text: initiated by NADPH oxidases and by enhancement of lipid peroxidation
                      - superscript [ref=e1146]:
                        - link "287" [ref=e1147] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R287"
                        - text: ","
                        - link "288" [ref=e1148] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R288"
                      - text: . In aerobic training, systemic redox communication is mediated by circulating extracellular vesicles
                      - superscript [ref=e1149]:
                        - link "289" [ref=e1150] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R289"
                      - text: . Circulating factors, such as growth factors and cytokines, collectively termed exerkines, mediate the exercise effects on redox homeostasis
                      - superscript [ref=e1151]:
                        - link "290" [ref=e1152] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R290"
                        - text: ","
                        - link "291" [ref=e1153] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#R291"
                      - text: .
              - generic [ref=e1154]:
                - heading "Conclusions and perspectives" [level=2] [ref=e1155]: Conclusions and perspectives
                - paragraph [ref=e1156]:
                  - text: In elaborating the fundamentals of redox regulation, this Review is structured to provide an outline of the underlying principles of how cellular functions are maintained in aerobic metabolism. For continuous functioning, cells and the whole organism use an orchestrated system of redox control, which includes multifaceted input cues that cause a redox signal to be generated, with subsequent transduction to specific target sites. These, in turn, activate the genome, epigenome, proteome and other ‘-omes’ to produce a functional response outcome in a concerted network activity. The general set-up (
                  - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1157] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#F1"
                  - text: ) is completed by appropriate feedback systems, which reinforce or desensitize the redox signal, the detailed mechanisms of which still need to be uncovered. This condition is called homeodynamics in view of the dynamic nature of the ongoing activity of this concerted effort, also implicated by the term eustress. The biochemistry of redox signalling through post-translational oxidative modification of protein cysteines (redox proteome) and the mechanism of gene activation through redox-sensitive transcription factors (
                  - link "Table 1" [ref=e1158] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#T1"
                  - text: ) have largely been elucidated. Also, the pathways of intracellular and intercellular redox communication are becoming more clearly known, as novel interactions of subcellular organelles have been discovered (
                  - link "Fig. 3" [ref=e1159] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#F3"
                  - text: ). The set of principles underlying the organization of cellular redox reactions, the redox code (
                  - link "Box 1" [ref=e1160] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#BX1"
                  - text: ), describes the contribution of redox regulation to life processes
                  - superscript [ref=e1161]:
                    - link "22" [ref=e1162] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R22"
                  - text: .
                - paragraph [ref=e1163]:
                  - text: However, several key questions remain to be answered. One major question concerns the hierarchy of redox regulation. As mentioned in the published literature and in several instances in the present Review, there are the so-called master regulators, including the endoplasmic reticulum
                  - superscript [ref=e1164]:
                    - link "88" [ref=e1165] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R88"
                  - text: ", ncRNAs"
                  - superscript [ref=e1166]:
                    - link "111" [ref=e1167] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R111"
                  - text: ", AHR"
                  - superscript [ref=e1168]:
                    - link "216" [ref=e1169] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R216"
                  - text: ", nucleoredoxin"
                  - superscript [ref=e1170]:
                    - link "104" [ref=e1171] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R104"
                  - text: ", PGC-1α"
                  - superscript [ref=e1172]:
                    - link "217" [ref=e1173] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R217"
                  - text: ", miRNA"
                  - superscript [ref=e1174]:
                    - link "218" [ref=e1175] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R218"
                  - text: "and so on, but the question arises: who regulates the regulator? Or, in other words, who watches the ‘watchmen’"
                  - superscript [ref=e1176]:
                    - link "219" [ref=e1177] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R219"
                  - text: "? How exactly does the overall orchestration operate to integrate the diverse signals into a common response, that is, what principles underlie hormesis? What governs the ‘set point’? How is a ‘tipping point’ identified, for example, for the decision between health outcome and cell death pathways? A better understanding will require network analysis in terms of feedback and feedforward loops at spatiotemporal patterns, the gene expression feedback loops"
                  - superscript [ref=e1178]:
                    - link "220" [ref=e1179] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R220"
                  - text: . A further dimension to consider is the role of redox regulation in its interplay with other main modes of metabolic regulation such as phosphorylation and/or dephosphorylation, calcium signalling and electrochemical signalling.
                - paragraph [ref=e1180]:
                  - text: Another emerging field of research concerns the more fine-grained interactions between the various types of reactive species, ROS, RNS, RSS and so on. H
                  - subscript [ref=e1181]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e1182]: "2"
                  - text: as a major redox signalling agent has been the main focus in this Review, but a wealth of new insight is emerging on other ROS such as superoxide together with nitric oxide
                  - superscript [ref=e1183]:
                    - link "10" [ref=e1184] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R10"
                  - text: ", singlet molecular oxygen"
                  - superscript [ref=e1185]:
                    - link "221" [ref=e1186] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R221"
                  - text: ", excited carbonyls"
                  - superscript [ref=e1187]:
                    - link "222" [ref=e1188] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R222"
                  - text: ", lipid hydroperoxides"
                  - superscript [ref=e1189]:
                    - link "223" [ref=e1190] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R223"
                  - text: or the role of persulfidation reactions in redox regulation
                  - superscript [ref=e1191]:
                    - link "224" [ref=e1192] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R224"
                  - text: .
                - paragraph [ref=e1193]:
                  - text: Physiological regulation of redox processes, as laid out in this Review, underlies the essential features of health
                  - superscript [ref=e1194]:
                    - link "225" [ref=e1195] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R225"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "226" [ref=e1196] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R226"
                  - text: and of the healthy ageing process
                  - superscript [ref=e1197]:
                    - link "227" [ref=e1198] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R227"
                  - text: . Distinct from this, disease processes are caused by or are associated with redox imbalance and redox distress. This rapidly growing research field is not covered here in detail. Aspects of redox medicine pertaining to a select number of organ systems (for example, cardiological, neurological, immunological, metabolic, skeletal muscle, cancer, ageing and neurodegenerative diseases) have been reviewed elsewhere
                  - superscript [ref=e1199]:
                    - link "4" [ref=e1200] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R4"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "9" [ref=e1201] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R9"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "228" [ref=e1202] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R228"
                    - text: –
                    - link "230" [ref=e1203] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R230"
                  - text: . Redox control in a cell-specific and site-specific manner at appropriate spatiotemporal delivery will contribute to precision redox medicine
                  - superscript [ref=e1204]:
                    - link "231" [ref=e1205] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R231"
                  - text: . The pharmacology of redox processes is shifting its orientation from addressing single molecular targets towards network medicine, applying systems biology paradigms to causal mechanisms rather than treating symptoms
                  - superscript [ref=e1206]:
                    - link "232" [ref=e1207] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R232"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "233" [ref=e1208] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R233"
                  - text: . This depends on a better understanding of mechanisms of antioxidant action
                  - superscript [ref=e1209]:
                    - link "234" [ref=e1210] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R234"
                  - text: . A major research challenge exists in further elucidating the molecular details of exposome–genome interactions from lifelong environmental, nutritional, microbiome and infectious exposures.
              - generic [ref=e1211]:
                - heading "Acknowledgements" [level=2] [ref=e1212]: Acknowledgements
                - paragraph [ref=e1213]: The authors are grateful to D. P. Jones, a longtime friend and colleague, for his substantial input and advice. B. Oleson helped with preparing figures. The authors also thank many researchers active in redox biology, who have generated an impressive literature that could not be covered exhaustively in the present overview. H.S. acknowledges longstanding support by Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft, Bonn, Germany, and by the National Foundation for Cancer Research, Bethesda, MD, USA. R.J.M. acknowledges Natural Sciences and Engineering Research Council of Canada Discovery Grant Program (RGPIN-2022-03240). U.J. acknowledges the National Institute of Health grant R35 GM122506 and the National Institute of Aging grant R21 AG078540.
              - generic [ref=e1214]:
                - heading "Glossary" [level=2] [ref=e1215]: Glossary
                - generic [ref=e1216]:
                  - term [ref=e1217]: Antioxidants
                  - definition [ref=e1218]:
                    - paragraph [ref=e1219]: Any substance that prevents, delays or removes oxidative damage. In biology, this includes various antioxidant enzymes and low-molecular-weight antioxidant compounds.
                  - term [ref=e1220]: Hormesis
                  - definition [ref=e1221]:
                    - paragraph [ref=e1222]: This term describes a biphasic dose–response phenomenon, whereby low-dose exposure leads to stimulation or preconditioning and high-dose exposure causes inhibition. The phenomenon is often described by a J-shaped or inverted U-shaped dose–response curve.
                  - term [ref=e1223]: Oxidative distress
                  - definition [ref=e1224]:
                    - paragraph [ref=e1225]: Biomolecular damage upon supraphysiological exposure to oxidants, resulting in detrimental consequence to life processes.
                  - term [ref=e1226]: p53 pathway
                  - definition [ref=e1227]:
                    - paragraph [ref=e1228]: A tumour suppression pathway, which oversees cell cycle regulation, DNA repair and cell death mechanisms to halt the proliferation of aberrant cells.
                  - term [ref=e1229]: Redox stress signalling threshold (RST)
                  - definition [ref=e1230]:
                    - paragraph [ref=e1231]: Condition at which the oxidant or reductant load causes the transition from beneficial (eustress) to detrimental (distress) outcome.
                  - term [ref=e1232]: Surface-enhanced Raman spectroscopy (SERS)
                  - definition [ref=e1233]:
                    - paragraph [ref=e1234]: A surface-sensitive method that amplifies Raman scattering from molecules absorbed on metal surfaces or nanostructures.
              - generic [ref=e1235]:
